#7412. Google Gemini Business Profile Tools Are Coming To Workspace via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Where search leaders are putting 2027 budget, plus the AI visibility scorecard that replaces session reporting.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Google says Gemini's Business Profile tools are coming to Workspace accounts and moves the Maps Message button. Its help page no longer lists region limits.

Google says businesses that manage their Business Profile with a Google Workspace account are getting access to Gemini’s profile tools. Its latest Small Business Bulletin says that access is rolling out now.

In contrast to the bulletin, Google’s help page regarding the Gemini connection shows work accounts still can’t be used. Google has removed the exclusion for the European Economic Area and the U.K. from the list of unsupported areas since Oct. 13.

Lisa Landsman, Google’s Head of Industry Engagement & SMB Success, posted the bulletin in the Business Profile Community on Nov. 5.

The same bulletin states that businesses who have established a chat option on their Business Profile now display a “Message” button next to the “Call” button on their Google Maps listings on both Android and iOS devices. Businesses may choose to provide a link to customers to send messages via WhatsApp. However, a dead link or unread inbox now sits in one of the top buttons displayed when a customer clicks on your Google Maps listing.

The bulletin labels Workspace access “rolling out now” for the Business Profile connection Google announced in June. Instead of an individual Gmail account, businesses can soon use a corporate Workspace email to link a verified Business Profile (through Gemini), Google says. Currently, a Workspace admin needs to enable Gemini app access within the Admin console before employees will be able to link.

Owners can also upload photos and videos into Gemini chat directly from a device or from Google Photos, Google says, allowing them to add images to their Business Profile photo or create new posts. Examples of prompts include setting your special hours for Black Friday.

The help center indicates that as of Nov. 6 owners need to log in using a personal Google Account, and it also states, “For now, this feature isn’t available if you sign in with a work or school Google Account.” Additionally, the media section does describe how users can upload images from a public URL, but it doesn’t indicate that users can load files from their device.

A previously archived version of the same help page dated Oct. 13 shows that at that time the Gemini connection was not available in the European Economic Area (EEA), nor the United Kingdom; in addition, 12 different languages were supported. However, the current help page does not list either restriction; it simply states, “Available wherever the Gemini web app is supported.” The most recent edition of Google’s newsletter does not address any region or language restrictions.

Google still restricts linking to an owner or manager of a single verified Business Profile, and only through the Gemini web app.

Landsman noted that Google has moved the Business Profile Message button higher up on Google Maps listings:

We’ve expanded the feature so customers on both Android and iOS can now find a prominent Message button right next to “Call” on your Google Maps listing. Previously, finding chat on Maps required scrolling deep down into your profile. Now, whether a customer views your profile, opens the expanded header, or uses the bottom action bar, messaging is front and center.

Tapping the button sends customers to the chat option the business has set up, including WhatsApp and text, according to the newsletter. Google’s chat help page lists those two options and says a profile with both set shows customers only the text option. The same page says chat options are available in select regions, without naming them, and might not be available for every profile.

Business Profile’s hours editor now has a “Generate hours” section, where owners type their schedule in plain language and Google fills in the hours fields. Google’s examples include appointment-only days and a lunch closing.

Google’s hours help page explains that the tool is available in select regions and languages, and owners can review and edit the generated hours before saving them.

Restaurants may turn off certain third-party ordering services by using the Business Profile dashboard (as I reported on Oct. 28). According to Google’s ordering help page, an order service is only hidden entirely when it’s turned off for both pickup and delivery.

In addition to Vegetarian and Vegan labels, there is a menu tag option for Dairy-Free and Gluten-Free labels. In addition, there are Kosher and Halal labeling options, which according to Google should only be used where certified.

Before submitting an appeal, you can upload supporting documentation into the appeals tool. As stated in Google’s help page, evidence is optional, and you can only submit an appeal once.

Currently, the Gemini connection doesn’t work for your agency’s multiple client profiles with a single login; you have to be managing just one verified profile. As of the new help page (that doesn’t exclude businesses in the EEA and the U.K.), businesses in these regions can check if they see their connection.

Before the holiday rush, send a test message through the chat link on every profile you manage.

Google’s bulletin doesn’t specify when Workspace access will reach every account. Keep an eye on the Gemini help page for updates to its personal-account rule or one-profile limit.

#7408. Reddit tests ads in search results

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The platform wants to help advertisers align their promotions with users’ discovery behavior even as the company’s overall search traffic drops.

Reddit is testing ads in search results in the app, as the platform looks for more ways to help advertisers align with discovery behaviors.

As shown in these example screens, Reddit’s search ads will be targeted by query, and will include a small ad marker in the upper section of the panel.

As explained by Reddit: “People come to Reddit seeking human perspectives and experiences they can’t find elsewhere, with half of Reddit users saying the platform helps them discover something new. As we continue improving the user search experience on Reddit, we’re also beginning to test ad placements in search. These placements leverage existing ad targeting in search results pages on Reddit, which have high purchase and search intent.”

Reddit said the promotions will be shown in search results based on a range of factors, though these will primarily be aligned with contextual relevance.

Reddit has been working to improve its discovery tools as the app tries to help users find more relevant matches for their queries.

In July, Reddit published a study that found that 72% of its users come to the platform seeking information, and 64% of them have a specific goal in mind.

That also aligns with how Reddit gains traffic from search results, since the platform gets significant citation volume from AI chatbots.

However, AI traffic has started to dry up. Last week, as part of its Q3 performance update, Reddit CEO Steve Huffman said that the company’s search traffic dropped by more than 1 million referrals per week on average in October.

That could reduce the value of its own in-app discovery process, though Tuesday’s announcement suggests that Reddit is clearly hoping to maintain its value as a product discovery tool.

Reddit said that it will initially test ads in search with a select group of eligible advertisers.

#7407. Brave Sells Its Search Index To AI, And Your Website Is Probably In It via @sejournal, @slobodanmanic

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Where search leaders are putting 2027 budget, plus the AI visibility scorecard that replaces session reporting.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

The New York Times says Brave copied 30 billion pages and sold them to AI companies. Your website is probably in that index.

You’ve probably never used Brave Search. Well, not directly. But the AI you ask questions might have, and it might have found your website there. On Nov. 2, the New York Times sued Brave, the company behind the Brave browser and Brave Search. The Times says Brave copied more than 30 billion webpages “without identifying itself or providing website owners with any mechanism to block its crawler,” then sold that content to AI companies. Brave hasn’t answered yet, so for now those are claims. But why can’t you keep its crawler out without keeping Google out too? Brave explains that itself, right there on its help page.

Brave sells access to its search index to AI companies, through a product called the Brave Search API. When an AI assistant needs something recent from the web, it doesn’t always go and read the web itself. It can pay to search someone else’s index instead. Brave’s About page says the API is “Used by some of the biggest names in AI, including Cohere, Mistral, Perplexity, and You.com.” Its AI page says the index holds “over 30 billion pages, with millions of new pages indexed each day.”

The Times’s complaint opens by naming five AI companies it says Brave supplies: Anthropic, Perplexity, Mistral, xAI and Cohere. None of them is being sued. Claude is the fuzzy one, because the evidence is old. The complaint points to a 2023 Brave post about Anthropic training Claude 2. In March 2025 Anthropic added Brave Search to its list of subprocessors, the outside companies it uses, which TechCrunch reported. The same week, Simon Willison, a developer who writes a widely read blog about AI tools, asked Claude to search for pelican facts, and the 10 results it cited matched the same search on Brave exactly. Whether Claude still uses Brave today, I couldn’t confirm.

Websites decide which crawlers may read them in a small file called robots.txt, and that only works because crawlers have names. OpenAI and Anthropic each publish the names of theirs, so you can let one company’s crawlers in and turn the other’s away.

Brave’s help page, the one at the top of this article, says its crawler “does not advertise a differentiated user agent.” In plain words, Brave’s crawler doesn’t wear a name tag, and Brave gives a reason: Some websites let Google in and nobody else, so a crawler with Brave’s name on it would get turned away. Brave says it follows Google’s rules instead: whatever Googlebot isn’t allowed to read, Brave won’t read either.

So you can’t let Google in and keep Brave out. You get both, or neither. To get out of Brave’s index, the same page points you to the noindex tag, which tells Google to drop the page too. The Times calls this Brave’s “refusal to release the name of its web crawler bot.”

A Brave search for site:nohacks.co on Nov. 3 showed 16 of my pages on the first page of results, 13 of them articles.

Brave’s result even shows the homepage description I replaced earlier that same day, so what Brave serves is a stored copy, not a live look at my website. My robots.txt names 13 AI bots, from GPTBot to Bytespider, and blocks two of them, Meta’s and ByteDance’s. There’s no line I could add for Brave. It’s hard to block a name that doesn’t exist.

Checking yours takes about 30 seconds: go to search.brave.com and type site: and your domain. Everything that shows up is in the index Brave sells.

Being in a search engine is a deal every website understands. Someone searches, sees your page, clicks, and lands on your website. Brave Search’s ordinary results work like that, though Brave has added AI answers of its own, called Ask Brave, which The Times is also suing over.

Selling the index is a different deal. The complaint quotes Brave describing one part of it, “LLM Context,” as handing over “extracted page chunks and source metadata in a shape and length that satisfies the needs of an LLM without need for further scraping.” So the AI company gets your page, already chopped up, and writes an answer from it. Whether that answer sends anyone back to you is the AI company’s call. It isn’t yours, and it isn’t something Brave is selling. That’s the part that bothers me.

The New York Times is claiming copyright infringement and getting around its paywall, among other things, and the Daily News, the Chicago Tribune and the Orlando Sentinel filed a separate case the same day. Brave hadn’t responded to either when I wrote this. It has argued its side before, though: when News Corp sent it a cease-and-desist, Brave sued first, saying it was only indexing the web, which “all search engine operators must do to exist,” and in July 2026 News Corp sued back, saying Brave hides its crawlers so publishers can’t detect or block them, Semafor reported. A ruling on The Times’s pages won’t automatically cover anyone else’s, but it will say a lot about every website in that index, and that’s probably yours too. So I’ll be watching this one.

This post was originally published on No Hacks.

We are currently hiring Staff Engineers for various projects within the Imunify360 product.

Imunify360 is CloudLinux's multi-layer security suite for Linux web servers: antivirus and malware cleanup, WAF, IDS/IPS, Proactive Defense, patch management (KernelCare/Patchman), and IP/domain reputation. It runs on hundreds of thousands of shared, VPS and dedicated hosting servers, under cPanel, Plesk, DirectAdmin and standalone.

Check out our website for more information: https://imunify360.com/

Most complex technical challenges in Imunify360 go beyond individual squads. A single detection change can impact the scanner, agent, UI, reputation backend, and release process. While squad leads own delivery within their teams, the Staff Engineer ensures technical alignment across the product.

As a Staff Engineer, you'll be a hands-on technical leader working across the entire product, focusing on the most critical and high-impact challenges as priorities evolve. You'll drive technical decisions, improve architecture, establish engineering standards, and help teams build sustainable solutions.

This is an individual contributor role, without direct people management responsibilities. You'll remain actively involved in development, spending approximately 30–50% of your time writing production code, rather than focusing solely on architectural design.

#7401. Google Rank Data Issues, Lighthouse Update & AI Opt-Out – SEO Pulse via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Where search leaders are putting 2027 budget, plus the AI visibility scorecard that replaces session reporting.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Rank data may carry one-word results and redirect URLs. Other updates cover PageSpeed lab scores, a shared AI training opt-out, technical SEO and AI search.

Welcome to the week’s Pulse: what you need to know about updates that are affecting how much you can rely on rank tracking data, why PageSpeed scores can go up or down on a page that has no changes and what a shared robots.txt control would mean in terms of preferences for AI training.

Here’s what matters for you and your work.

Two issues in the Google results that rank tracking tools collect can throw off ranking reports. Unrelated listings can crowd a tracked page out of the collected results, and unresolved goto links can hide which page ranked.

In an SE Ranking review of 10,000 Google US desktop keywords published Nov. 3, 84 percent of the flagged cases were unrelated listings about a single word from the query. Of those unrelated blocks, 96.5% began at position 9 or below. A related pattern is tracked by Nozzle using a different methodology and it reports its weekly share of impacted SERPs had fallen to 1.5% in mid-October, then rose to 7.6% during the week of Oct. 26. DataForSEO states Google has implemented a mechanism to restrict the amount of goto links from a single results page that can be resolved over a given timeframe. As such, they state their High Priority and Live modes will now only resolve approximately half of goto URLs. The status page of Serper lists the limitation of goto URLs as being on Oct. 30, while SerpApi’s second incident of goto links replacing direct links, which opened on Nov. 3, still showed as Identified when last reviewed on Nov. 4.

Check if you were dropped at a position of 9 or lower from late September on to see if your replacement URLs are about one word of your query. Also, starting with about Oct. 30 (if it was a Google redirect) compare the URL that your tracker stored versus where the redirect points. To verify which factor caused an issue use Search Console’s impression/clicks reports for the same query, as SE Ranking recommends.

Read our full coverage: Rank Trackers Find Google Results About One Word Of A Query and SERP Data Providers Say Google Is Throttling Goto Result Links

PageSpeed Insights now runs Lighthouse 13.6.0, which updates the loading simulation used for its lab scores. As a result, your page’s score may change even if you haven’t changed anything on the page.

According to a post by Google’s Barry Pollard on Bluesky, the updated version of Lighthouse’s simulated FCP (First Contentful Paint) and LCP (Largest Contentful Paint) will be more accurate and will generally provide better results. He also stated that JavaScript-intensive websites would be more likely to experience the impact of this new version. The simulation used as the basis for these predictions, called Lantern, has been modified to use Chrome’s render-blocking label for every individual request made. It then defaults to using the fetch priority value if the render-blocking label cannot be found. In addition, the field data provided in the same report was based upon actual user data collected from the real world, not as part of a synthetic test like Lighthouse.

The pre- and post-switch lab scores aren’t generated in the same simulation; therefore, some of the increase you see on pages you’ve optimized might be from the new version. Make sure each report lists the same Lighthouse version, and compare your field data, before claiming an improvement.

Read our full coverage: Google Lighthouse Update Changes How PageSpeed Insights Simulates Loading

Google, Microsoft, and OpenAI announced on Oct. 30 that they plan to honor a shared robots.txt control for AI training preferences, based on an Internet-Draft by Anthropic’s Kevin Kelley.

The control is a normal robots.txt group under a policy token called __usage_ai-training, that has both Allow & Disallow rules; at this time none of these three organizations support the control, according to their publicly announced timelines. Google’s post states that it plans to treat a shared opt-out as though a site opted out of Google-Extended, when the site does not have Google-Extended defined. In addition, Google’s crawler documentation states that Google-Extended also covers grounding in Gemini Apps. Microsoft will utilize the shared control for training purposes, will introduce a separate page-level AI Grounding preference, and will cease treating NoArchive and NoCache as training and/or grounding preferences after March 1, 2027.

A site would be able to use one robots.txt group as replacement for many per-company training rules after the companies read it; however, opting out through that group would not cover the same uses at Google and Microsoft. The documentation provided by Google regarding its robots.txt controls does not separate Gemini training from Gemini Apps’ grounding. Companies using NoArchive and/or NoCache in conjunction with Microsoft have until March 1, 2027 to switch to the new preferences.

Read our full coverage: Google, Microsoft & OpenAI Plan To Honor Shared AI Training Opt-Out

Google’s John Mueller said AI systems “build on SEO” and that he expects technical SEO in particular to stay relevant. He spoke with David Iwanow in an interview posted Oct. 29 from Search Central Deep Dive Europe 2026.

Mueller also stated that AI systems crawl sites in slightly different ways using intent based on things like training versus getting the latest content; he described this as “another flavor of technical SEO”. When asked about listicle formats Mueller indicated that when tactics are effective they are likely to be mimicked poorly until the algorithms adapt and therefore establishing a solid foundation for a website is your best bet.

Mueller placed AI crawling within technical SEO, so you can include how AI crawlers request your pages in the technical audits you already run. Just be sure to check each crawler’s access and robots.txt rules individually.

Read our full coverage: Google Says Technical SEO Remains Key For AI SEO

ChatGPT consumer product lead Adam Fry told Big Technology that ChatGPT’s “volume of search-like queries is now roughly one-fifth the volume of queries of Google”.

Fry noted each question that sends ChatGPT to the web is considered a search by ChatGPT’s developer; however, according to the article, there is no Google figure, period or market given for this comparison. Ahrefs’ referral tracker measures the source of referrals at the site level and shows Google accounts for 24.74% of tracked traffic across 118,671 sites during October with ChatGPT accounting for 0.36%.

OpenAI’s ratio counts questions that send ChatGPT to the web, while referral data tracks visits sent to websites. To keep your channel plan clear, name the measure behind any ChatGPT size figure, because search counts and referral shares describe different things.

Read our full coverage: Google’s Search Volume Is About 5x ChatGPT’s, OpenAI Says

A number of statistics reported by SEO teams this week require an assessment of how these statistics are collected prior to being viewed as information regarding performance for a website or channel. This week’s issue with rank data is the collection of one word results and redirect URLs; a simulated PageSpeed lab score will be affected when using a new simulation versus the previous simulation; and the size of ChatGPT will depend upon if you use search volume or total visits to determine its size. On the other hand, the planned shared opt-out would cover different uses based upon which company reads it; Mueller defined AI crawling, with its different intents, as technical SEO. When reporting changes in metrics or data from reports, this is where you should document which tracking tool or version of a tool was used as well as the criteria used (definition) to produce the report.

#7399. Instagram tries to entice long-form creators

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The platform is using one-off payouts and premium placements to make up for its lack of the type of robust payout system offered by YouTube.

As Instagram makes its new push into long-form content via Features, the platform is also working on other ways to promote long-form video in order to get more people viewing and more creators posting.

A key question from creators, however, has been around monetization, and why they would want to post their longer videos to Instagram, given that its payout system is nowhere near as lucrative as YouTube’s Partner Program.

But even without a solidified payments system in place, Instagram does have some other tricks up its sleeve to get creators sharing more long-form videos.

As shown in these screenshots posted by creator economy expert Lia Haberman, Instagram is highlighting long-form video in a prominent space at the top of its Explore page for some users. That will certainly get the attention of creators, who will see this as a massive opportunity to get more people checking out their content.

Instagram is also reportedly offering one-off payments of up to $500,000 to a selection of big-name creators to get them posting long-form content.

In combination, it seems like Instagram will be able to make Features a bigger consideration for creators, even without a more competitive monetization program.

Though the platform is also working on that element.

Instagram Chief Adam Mosseri has acknowledged the app will have to evolve its payment program to incentivize long-form creators, but it hasn’t worked out the best way to move forward yet.

In an interview with YouTubers Colin and Samir published last week, Mosseri discussed the various challenges Instagram is facing in building a sustainable monetization program.

Mosseri said the aim of adding long-form content on IG was to compete with YouTube, which is now seeing most of its viewership growth via Connected TV, or people watching YouTube videos on their home TV sets. Mosseri said that Instagram, ideally, will be able to integrate short and long-form content into a more Instagram-style approach, where long-form videos can be split into short Reels, thereby providing viewers multiple consumption options.

But on payments, Mosseri said that Instagram was still weighing its options.

Mosseri added that he doesn’t believe platform payments, performance-based incentives or creator bonuses are a revenue driver. Inserting mid-roll ads can also be disruptive, and while Mosseri said Instagram is making one-off payments to bootstrap Features, that isn’t a sustainable path.

Which leaves few options, outside of helping creators organize brand deals to support their content. Mosseri said that Instagram offers certain smaller creator payment schemes, but those have also been historically cost-negative and a losing bet in terms of ROI.

Mosseri’s comments likely won’t give a lot of hope to creators looking to make more money via long-form content in the app. This is especially true given that an external analysis of Instagram’s payout program, conducted by VidIQ, shows that creators on Instagram generally only earn around 10% of what they make on YouTube.

That could eventually see Features going the same way as IGTV, the platform’s previous long-form content play. IGTV also died because there was no financial incentive for creators to post to Instagram instead of posting to YouTube.

Given Mosseri’s comments, it’s hard to see this outcome shifting , even with expanded exposure options.

#7394. Facebook expands Creator Fast Track program to Brazil

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The program offers incentives for creators not yet posting to Facebook, though the monetary incentives for Brazilian creators are significantly less than what the company offered in North America.

Meta wants to get more creators in Brazil posting to Facebook, so it’s expanding its Creator Fast Track program, which offers guaranteed monthly payments to entice creators who are already popular on other apps to share their content to Facebook as well.

Launched in the U.S. and Canada back in March, the Facebook Creator Fast Track program is aimed at creators who’ve already built an audience elsewhere.

To qualify for Brazil’s version of the program, local creators will need to have at least 20,000 followers on either Instagram, TikTok or YouTube, and have generated at least 30,000 video views over the previous 30 days.

The program is open to creators who haven’t posted a Facebook Reel in the last 6 months and is only valid for “high-quality, original” content.

Those accepted into the program will be required to post at least 20 original Reels on Facebook during each 30-day period over the course of three months. Those Reels need to be posted on separate days, and the videos must be original to Facebook. Creators can re-post content that they’ve shared in other apps, so long as it hasn’t been previously posted on their Facebook Page.

Creators who meet these requirements will be rewarded with guaranteed payments not tied to video performance, but instead linked to audience counts on other apps.

As explained by Meta: “Your monthly payment depends on the highest follower count on one eligible Instagram, TikTok or YouTube account linked during your application. The program uses only your highest follower count from a single account.”

Creators with up to 100,000 followers in one of these apps are eligible to receive $25 per 30-day period, and up to $75 total for the life of the program. Creators with one million followers or more on these other platforms will get $300 per day, up to $900.

Those amounts are significantly lower than what Meta offered creators in the U.S. and Canada in the first iteration of the program.

In the first iteration, creators in the U.S. and Canada with up 100,000 followers were offered up to $450 per month, while those with a million or more followers got $3,000 monthly payments.

It seems that Meta has watered down those amounts for the Brazilian market, which could suggest that the initial scheme didn’t deliver enough return on that outlay to justify the payouts.

Yet, at these lower amounts, it seems unlikely this will be a big lure for creators in Brazil, who, in most cases, are earning significantly more from monetization programs on other apps.

But clearly, Meta is keen to make Facebook more engaging, and by enticing established creators over, it’s hoping that it can then keep them posting to the app by showcasing the value of connecting with the Facebook audience, and tapping into increased video engagement in the app.

As part of its Q1 2026 earnings announcement, Meta reported that total video watch time on Facebook increased by more than 8% globally, representing the largest quarter-over-quarter engagement gain in four years.

#7393. Snapchat posts modest user increase in Q3

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Company revenue was up, but the platform’s daily active user growth has stalled across nearly every market.

Snap Inc. posted its Q3 2026 earnings report on Wednesday, which showed that user growth on Snapchat has largely stalled, while overall company revenue saw a steady year-over-year increase.

First, on users. Snapchat now has 495 million daily active users, up from 493 million in Q2.

An increase of just 2 million daily actives, quarter over quarter, is not a particularly positive endorsement of the app’s expanded market opportunities. But even more pressing, Snapchat saw no growth at all in daily active users in either the North American or European markets, meaning all user growth came from its Rest of World segment.

This could end up having a broader impact on the platform’s revenue potential, because Snapchat generates the majority of its money in North America and Europe.

Indeed, Snapchat’s revenue per user in North America was $11.07 in Q3, up from $10.26 the previous quarter. By contrast, the platform only generated $1.13 per user in the Rest of World. That means that Snapchat is only seeing user growth in regions where it earns 90% less per user, which is based primarily on ad intake.

It's possible that Snapchat could also be hit harder than other apps by expanded teen social media bans, which are being considered or implemented in multiple markets. According to Pew Research published last year, around 55% of U.S. teens between ages 13 and 17 use Snapchat, making it the fourth most popular platform among this cohort, after YouTube, TikTok and Instagram.

In Australia, which implemented social media usage restrictions for children under age 16 in December 2025, Snapchat said it subsequently locked or disabled more than 415,000 user accounts. These accounts, per Snapchat, had either been openly declared as belonging to a user under age 16, or Snapchat determined that was the case based on its age detection technology.

Snapchat said in August 2023 that it had more than 8 million users in Australia. That means the teen social media bans may have cost the platform around 5% of its total user base in that market.

Which may not seem like a major shift, but if Snapchat loses 5% of its users across the world, that would be significant, especially given its already shrinking growth margins. In addition, teen bans could also lead to Snapchat losing some of its value as the connection tool for younger audiences.

On the positive side, in his prepared remarks to accompny the earnings report, Snap CEO Evan Spiegel said that average daily messages sent in the app increased 8% year over year in Q3, while the number of people engaging in two-way communication increased 6% year over year for the same period.

Spiegel also noted that the number of people posting to Spotlight grew 130% year over year, while daily active viewers increased 40% year over year, underlining the enduring value of short-form video content.

On the revenue side, the company's intake rose 14% year-over-year to $1.72 billion.

By region, overall third-quarter revenue in North America was up 13% year over year to $1.01 billion, while Europe was up 22 % to $363 million, and the Rest of World was up 11% to $345 million. Again, the revenue discrepancy between regions is notable here. Snapchat has three times more users in the Rest of World market versus EU, so while the revenue numbers between them are close, that doesn't equate to the same value, while it still generates the vast majority of its intake from the U.S.

Even so, the fact that Snap is seemingly succeeding in deriving more revenue from its key markets, despite no user growth, is also worthy of note.

In the accompanying earnings release, Spiegel said that artificial intelligence has become a significant advantage for the company’s teams and its ad platform.

Snap has also been working to evolve its business offerings, with improved creator partnership opportunities. The company also started pitching its ads to B2B brands.

Snap’s other big opportunity, per Spiegel, is its AR Specs, which launched in September. The $2,200 device is now available for pre-order in the U.S., France and the U.K., though it isn’t being shipped to users just yet.

Snap seems to hope the glasses will bring about new opportunities in advanced digital connection via wearable digital overlays.

But initial data in Snap’s own Q3 report showed that Specs sales haven’t contributed meaningfully to its bottom line yet. In a statement, Snap CFO Doug Hott said that advertising revenue grew 4% and the company’s “Other Revenue grew 84%,” led by Snapchat+, Memories Storage, and Lens+.

Overall, Snap’s third-quarter earnings data showed that the company still has a long way to go in securing its future prospects, and without any insight on the potential market for its AR Specs, it’s impossible to gauge consumer interest or the value of this element.

Subscription growth is relevant, but Snap appears to have a lot of other factors working against it that may impact its future results.

#7389. How Fresh Step used AI to go from brief to ads at one-tenth the cost

The Clorox cat litter brand worked with agency RECE to get a new campaign in market in about a month.

Since its launch in 1984, Fresh Step has been the crown jewel of Clorox’s cat litter portfolio, a segment of the CPG marketer’s business that has lagged for some time and does not command the share of the $5 billion U.S. cat litter market that executives would like. The company determined that part of the brand’s problem was heightened category pressure on its core promise around odor control.

“Perceptions of [Fresh Step] had actually started to dip. Not core perceptions — it was still good — but relative perceptions,” Eric Schwartz, CMO of The Clorox Company, said about Fresh Step during a presentation at the Association of National Advertisers Masters of Marketing conference last month.

To address those issues and reassert Fresh Step’s positioning as being superior in odor control, the brand needed a new campaign that would be memorable and engaging, but made quickly. The marketer wanted to be able to pivot easily, if needed, rather than be stuck after betting the farm on a creative concept.

“There's a real business pressure, and it was not going to be acceptable [to say] I'll come in six months with my idea,” Deb Crandall, senior marketing director on litter at The Clorox Company, said in an interview. “We need things in market ASAP.”

Fresh Step teamed with artificial intelligence-powered agency RECE — short for radical empathy and creative excellence — to go from brief to finished ad creative on three 15-second spots in 30 days and at one-tenth the cost of a traditional production (and without having to rely on finicky cat actors).

“The speed AI creates lets us bring strategy, concept, copy, direction, AI artists, editors and composers together, all at once. It’s closer to a tech startup’s prototyping process than a traditional ad agency’s,” Toygar Bazarkaya, founder and chief creative officer at RECE, said in emailed comments.

The ads are rolling out now across linear TV with cutdowns on social and will be live by Nov. 9.

“This campaign could not have existed in this way, probably even a year ago. That's how fast I think the advances [in AI] are coming,” Crandall said. “The technology unlocked some magic that wouldn't have been possible without it.”

Fresh Step’s new ads feature a cast of talking cats of different breeds with different accents and attitudes. The spots deliver the brand’s value proposition around odor in different ways: a dog can no longer smell its way to a litter box; a huge cat is no problem for the brand; and cats line up around the block to use Fresh Step.

The brief began as usual, with a business challenge and a consumer insight. AI helped to rapidly prototype creative concepts to show what was resonating, delivering emotion in a way that a script on the page can’t always do. Being able to see talking cats in action helped convince the brand that the ads would still connect with consumers.

“One of the fears was would AI cats give you that same emotive response as when you see a real animal? I think the work does deliver on that. It captures that emotional response,” Crandall said. “That inner part of the process — being able to see it and feel it like midstream — really helped us to unlock and push us into some new spaces that we hadn't been before as a brand.”

The fact that AI-powered content creation technology is trained on an internet that is still overloaded with cat videos may make it easier for the AI-created talking cats to cross the uncanny valley than AI-created humans. And while consumers are wary of AI in ads, the Fresh Step ads are clearly computer generated.

“As consumers, we're all experimenting with this technology in our lives and figuring out how it helps us and doesn't help us,” Crandall said. “This, to me, was very clearly AI. We're not trying to fool anybody here. We're using it to dramatize situations that many cat owners can relate to.”

The speed and cost efficiency of the AI-created campaign will help Fresh Step reinvest in the brand: Getting it done at one-tenth the cost roughly equates to being able to create 10 more campaigns.

“It's always a sign when you've hooked onto something when you can already think of the next five ideas that you want to go do. We have some other things that we're thinking about creating once we get these out there and see how they resonate,” Crandall said. “It's that beautiful blend of humor and connecting to real cat-parent truths that I think is the secret sauce.”

Plus, the creativity unlocked by technology is helping the brand to extend the campaign into what CMO Schwartz referred to as systems of relevance that continuously deliver organic content.

“The deliberately modular design lets us adapt benefits, innovations, packaging, even messages based on what we will learn about consumers in market,” the executive said of the campaign at the ANA event. “That's the real advantage. This isn't a piece of creative we had to bet big on, and now we let it fly and leave it alone. We can keep learning, refining and improving as we go.”

Meta has launched a desktop version of its Edits video editing app, with Mac users now able to download the tool.

The desktop version of Edits gives users a lot more screen space to work with, which should make it easier to utilize its various creation options.

As explained by Brett Westervelt, head of Edits: “We built the desktop editor with creators in mind. It has everything you need to make great videos for any platform, free and customizable by you. Creators get Instagram’s licensed and royalty-free audio libraries, hundreds of sound and video effects, and unlimited automatic captions that support multiple languages.”

Launched in January 2025, Edits was primarily designed to compete with ByteDance’s CapCut app, which offers similar video editing tools but more usage limitations. At the time, there was speculation about ByteDance’s future in the U.S., and Instagram saw the opportunity to launch Edits as a replacement. The tool’s easy integration with Instagram was also positioned as a way to drive more interest in posting short-form content to the app.

The desktop version could be its most significant update yet because the bigger layout makes it easier to access and action various options within the creation flow.

Westervelt said that any audio and text styles Edits users have saved on their mobile app will translate to the desktop version when they sign in. The company is also exploring cross-platform editing so users can keep working on projects in either app.

#7376. How marketers can better optimize for AI search visibility

Most of what marketers are expected to spend on artificial intelligence visibility in 2027 is misdirected, writes GeoSurge founder Francisco Vigo.

The following is a guest piece written by Francisco Vigo, co-founder and CEO of AI visibility company GeoSurge. Opinions are the author’s own.

Most of what marketers will spend on artificial intelligence visibility in 2027 is aimed at the wrong end of the process. The standard playbook tunes content for the moment that an assistant searches the web, assuming live search gives every brand a fair shot at being mentioned in AI results. By the time the model searches, though, it has already decided which brands are worth looking up, and it makes that call from memory.

We measured this at GeoSurge. Brands an AI model already remembered were searched about three times as often as brands it didn't, 55.7% versus 17.4%, and for its five most-remembered brands, the rate reached 67%. The pattern held in all nine industries we tested. If memory decides who gets searched, the budget belongs in what builds memory: the coverage, reviews and category association that models learn from between training runs.

When AI assistants first started answering buying questions, brands worried that the model's knowledge was frozen at training time. Live web search seemed to solve that, because whatever the model doesn't know, it can look up in real time. We call that RAG - Retrieval-Augmented Generation. However, before a model can retrieve anything, it has to decide what to search for, and it makes that decision using its own memory, somewhat like a human would.

Modern AI search doesn't run a single query. Ask "what's the best business checking account for a small company" and the model splits it into a batch of parallel searches. Some are generic and some name specific companies. Google calls this “query fan-out.” Every citation and recommendation flows through those queries, and a source that never matches one rarely enters the answer.

To see how memory shapes those queries, we first recorded the brands a model could recall with no web access, across nine industries. Then, over 12 days, we ran 66 realistic buyer questions 60 times each, capturing about 4,000 responses and 13,281 of the searches the model ran behind the scenes. About 69% of those searches were generic category queries. Of the 31% that named a brand, nearly two-thirds named one of the model's top-five remembered brands.

This makes sense mechanically. A model writing search queries draws on the same learned associations it uses to write anything else. It can't search for a brand it never learned, any more than you can Google a restaurant you've never heard of.

The effect isn't uniform, and that matters for marketers who are planning.

For example, in automotive, 82% of brand-specific searches named one of the model's top-five remembered brands; in finance, 77% did. In fitness and wellness, the figure was closer to 50%, because models searched beyond their memory far more often.

Our tentative read, which the study didn't test, is that memory-led categories are dominated by a few long-established, heavily covered brands. Search-led categories, by contrast, have many smaller, newer ones. Each industry rests on six to 12 prompts, so treat the splits as indicative. Still, the distinction is useful. It tells you whether your problem is retrievability, which content and technical work can fix relatively quickly, or memorability, which it can't.

First, diagnose before you spend. In a search-led category, well-structured and highly retrievable content can move results relatively quickly. In a memory-led category, the same work has a much lower ceiling.

Second, fund earned media as infrastructure. Model memory forms from the training corpus: years of press coverage, analyst reports, reviews, forum discussions and consistent association between a brand and its category. These disciplines were long justified in the soft language of “reputation.” Now they have a mechanical function, because they are how a brand gets written into the systems that decide what to search for. They can't be injected at query time; they accrue between training runs, or not at all.

Third, measure recall, not just citations. Counting citations measures the scoreboard, but the game is decided upstream, in what models remember before a single query fires. That scoreboard is also getting cheaper. Tracking that typically costs hundreds of dollars a month now has free options. If seeing the answer costs next to nothing, more of the budget can go toward shaping it.

None of this makes query-time optimization worthless. It works in search-led categories, and even in memory-led ones the generic queries leave room to compete.

But the industry has the priority order backward. The dominant playbook optimizes the last millisecond of a process that is substantially decided months earlier, inside a training run.

For challenger brands, this is urgent. Remembered brands get searched, searched brands get cited and those citations feed the corpus the next generation of models learns from. Each training cycle dominated by incumbents makes that loop harder to break.

You can't win at query time what you lost at training time; however, training time comes around again, and what the models remember next is being written now.

Get the free daily newsletter read by industry experts

Major marketers looking to engage U.S. consumers around the milestone must prove their bona fides or risk looking like bandwagoners.

As shown in these example screens, the TikTok Shop app is focused primarily on product listings and driving shopping behavior, although some TikTok video content is also accessible.

That’s been central to the company’s expanded success in China, where the local version of the app, called Douyin, now generates a significant amount of income from in-app sales.

According to KrAsia, Douyin generated nearly $500 billion in gross merchandise volume in 2025, with livestream commerce in particular, driving a significant portion of that activity.

TikTok has been trying to push shopping in its app as well in order to replicate the expanded revenue potential that Douyin has seen from integrating shopping into the video viewing experience.

This play has driven some success, though not on the same scale as in China.

According to Marketing Maze, TikTok globally generated $26.2 billion GMV in the first half of 2025.

Part of the challenge appears to be that TikTok users are seemingly less interested in shopping in-stream, preferring instead to visit separate apps. Shoppers in the U.S., for example, seem more habitually aligned to shopping on Amazon, while they watch videos on TikTok or YouTube. Virtually every social app has tried to merge the two, but none have succeeded in driving significant shopping activity.

Maybe the addition of a separate shopping app could better align with this behavior while still keeping shoppers within the TikTok ecosystem. That could also help to better associate TikTok’s brand with shopping activity and ultimately benefit its broader shopping push.

As reported by social media expert Matt Navarra, the TikTok Shop app is currently available in Singapore, Thailand, Malaysia and the Philippines.

#7370. Meta, TikTok and Google push back on California law

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The companies are arguing that a new law set to take effect next year violates First Amendment rights and oversteps regulatory boundaries, per Reuters.

Social media giants Meta and TikTok have got another major legal fight on their hands as they join Google in seeking to block the enforcement of a new law in California that would force them to stop using “psychologically exploitative features” on users under age 16.

California’s Assembly Bill 1709, which was signed into law by Governor Gavin Newsom on Sept. 10, says that online platforms operating in the state are required to restrict the usage of certain addictive features for users under age 16.

Per the text of the law, those addictive elements include “addictive feeds, endless scrolls, autoplay, and their functional equivalents, including any feature that learns from user information or behavior in order to prolong engagement with a particular internet website, online service, online application, or mobile application.”

Under this new law, social platforms are essentially required to either remove these elements from the accounts of underage users, or ban users under age 16 from using their platforms. The law is currently scheduled to come into effect on Jan. 1.

But as reported by Reuters, platforms are pushing back on these restrictions and arguing that they violate the First Amendment, as well as Section 230 protections for online providers.

As per Reuters: “In separate lawsuits on Wednesday, Google, Meta and TikTok ​said the law wrongly compels them to honor the state’s preferred means of presenting ⁠content to children, and substitutes the state’s judgment for that of parents.”

The argument these platforms are making is that California legislators are overstepping regulatory boundaries in order to impose restrictive operating conditions that infringe on users’ rights.

It’s the latest in a series of significant legal challenges relating to potential teen harm facing social media platforms.

In March, a jury in California found that apps including Facebook, Instagram and YouTube can have significant mental health impacts, particularly on younger users, due to intentionally designed, addictive systems. The jury ruled that both Meta and YouTube parent company Alphabet ignored these risks in order to maximize business opportunities.

Then in August, as part of a separate case, Meta agreed to pay more than $18 billion to halt a potentially damaging lawsuit over social media harms. As part of that settlement, Meta also agreed to implement new restrictions on teen social media use.

These cases have led to the implementation of California’s new law, which the platforms are now seeking to push back on in order to limit the impact of these findings.

Social platforms are also facing various new laws that would ban teens from using their apps entirely in many regions.

#7366. Meta makes Muse agents speak

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The artificial intelligence-powered tool will be able to recognize conversation queries and respond in a voice selected by the user.

Meta is giving its Muse AI agents speaking capabilities in an effort to make them more engaging and easier to use.

Muse users will be able to select a voice for their personal artificial intelligence-powered agent and empower audio queries.

As explained by Meta: “Sometimes it’s easier to think out loud. With voice, you can talk through an idea with your Muse and let it take shape without worrying about the exact prompt.”

Meta said this should make it easier to use Muse to plan different projects, including work-related prep.

“Some things are better rehearsed out loud,” the company said. “Prepare for a job interview, run through a presentation, or talk through a pitch — hearing a response and answering in the moment makes it feel more like the real thing.”

Meta said the Muse avatar will also be able to express more as it speaks, and will use subtle expressions and gestures that match the tone of the conversation.

“It reacts while you talk, so you can tell it’s following along,” Meta said. “And you choose how present you want it to be: voice only, the avatar alongside your conversation, or full screen when you want to talk face to face.”

Users will even be able to give their Muse characters a custom voice, like giving it a New York accent or making it speak like a pirate.

Which, for some reason, Meta is convinced is something that people want, despite similar options falling flat with users previously.

Back in 2023, Meta introduced celebrity-styled AI chatbots on Messenger, which enabled users to engage with AI bots that could reply, in text form, in the style of various celebrities.

Nobody cared, and less than a year later, Meta shut this feature down due to lack of interest.

But Meta wasn’t done. In August 2024, Meta launched a limited audio version of the same, with the company paying millions of dollars to record the voices of actors such as Judi Dench, Keegan-Michael Key and Awkwafina for AI chatbots.

That didn’t resonate either. Meanwhile, The Wall Street Journal reported in April 2025 that some users had used the feature to generate explicit content voiced by these celebrities. That ultimately led to the demise of the feature before an expanded roll-out.

The addition of custom voices for Muse bots isn’t exactly the same, but even so, Meta seems very dedicated to a novelty idea that nobody else seems to care about.

Either way, users can now give their Muse agent a voice, so that they can walk around in public looking totally normal as they talk out loud to an AI character on their phone.

#7363. Old Navy flies with celebs, Gap keeps it acapella for holiday campaigns

The sister brands are sticking to their respective marketing strengths as the holiday season kicks into high gear.

Old Navy has launched its holiday campaign with a star-studded spot, per details shared with Marketing Dive. Actors Alan Cumming and Meg Stalter serve as flight attendant and pilot, respectively, on the “Jingle Jet,” where passengers include Paris Hilton, Charli D'Amelio, actor True Whitaker and influencer Vidya Gopalan and her family.

The campaign follows the recent launch of a holiday campaign from sister brand Gap that is centered around music. Taken together, these see the brands sticking to their respective marketing strengths as the holiday kicks into high gear.

Old Navy’s campaign was created with the brand’s in-house team and agency BBH USA. It was directed by duo Adam & Dave, with Academy Award-winner Autumn Durald Arkapaw as director of photography. The ad spotlights fashions starting at $7.99 including sequined dresses, matching pajamas and performance fleece and ends with the value-oriented tagline “More Merry For Your Money.”

“At Old Navy, we believe the holidays should be filled with more fun, more style and more possibilities for everyone. From festive fashion to gifts for the whole family, we’re making it easier to celebrate big without compromising,” said Zac Posen, chief creative officer for the brand in press materials.

The campaign is the first effort launched during the tenure of Old Navy CEO Michael Francis, who stepped into the role on Nov. 2, and the latest example of how the retailer has changed its marketing strategy after a recent sales slump.

“Our marketing has been totally rewired … to create more sharper price points and more value orientation with, of course, the Old Navy narrative that keeps our brands really relevant,” Gap Inc. CEO Richard Dickson said during a Goldman Sachs conference in September.

The Old Navy holiday effort sees the brand re-team with Hilton, who featured in a summer campaign that drove engagement but not traffic, Dickson said at the conference. The brand’s last holiday campaign was in 2024 and starred Jennifer Hudson with a cast of lesser-known characters.

Gap on Nov. 3 rolled out the latest iteration of its “Give Your Gift” campaign, highlighting individuality, creativity and community in an ad that brings together three choirs to sing Jackson 5 classic "I'll Be There.”

“The spirit of this campaign celebrates unity and togetherness. That’s the sentiment we aim to bring to our customers this season, offering new opportunities to engage with our brand and create memorable experiences that bring Gap’s gifting destination to life, both digitally and IRL,” Gap CMO Fabiola Torres said in a statement.

The campaign spans digital, social, video and owned channels and will be extended through immersive shopping experiences, including a multiday pop-up opening in Williamsburg, Brooklyn, this month.

Gap launched the “Give Your Gift” campaign in 2024 with an ad that featured an a cappella version of Janet Jackson’s “Together Again” and returned in 2025 with an ad centered on a cover of Miley Cyrus song “The Climb.”

Gap Inc. comparable sales were down 1% year over year in the second quarter of 2026, with Old Navy down 4% and dragging down overall results at a time when the company's flagship brand Gap saw comp sales increase 10%. The company will release its third quarter earnings on Nov. 19.

#7359. Lenovo NA CMO on telling the ‘full stack story’ through sports

The World Cup drove 4.4 billion social impressions and a 4.2% lift in brand value for the tech company.

Lenovo used its FIFA sponsorship to put its artificial intelligence tools in front of billions of viewers. From the Lenovo AI Referee View to an ad campaign featuring soccer superstar David Beckham, the tech company looked to put itself front-and-center during one of the most watched sports programs in the world. And it worked, earning the brand 4.4 billion social impressions and 1.8 billion creator/influencer impressions, per data provided by the brand.

Over the course of the tournament, Lenovo said it saw a 4.2 times lift in brand consideration among tech-focused soccer fans and a 4.2% lift in brand value. It also claims to have captured the top competitive share of voice among FIFA partners on social media and driven 460,000 page views to Lenovo-owned sites.

Lenovo North America CMO Milo Speranzo sat down with Marketing Dive to talk about making the most of expensive sports sponsorships, how the brand is looking to replicate success with the women’s soccer tournament in 2027 and how it's differentiating itself in the AI race.

The following interview has been edited for brevity and clarity.

MILO SPERANZO: Having the same goals, the same messaging and storytelling, as well as the ability for us to integrate our product and technology into the actual event was a key pillar in that decision making of, do we go all in? With FIFA and the World Cup, there's no partial in, or at least doesn't feel like it.

It's an all-in engagement, and when we made that decision in 2024, we were very confident that we had covered all three of our determination silos that we look at in order to really ask ourselves: Will this be a successful partnership? [Those silos are] tech integration, can we tell an authentic story and then the marketing KPIs on the back half. Those three pillars for the World Cup just lined up perfectly.

Yes, it was. When we look at a return on investment for a partnership, the brand piece is a third of what we judge ourselves on. I would say that all of our marketing-specific KPIs were exceeded. It was very much an awareness play, as well as educating our customers that we are not only products. We are a very deeply resourced supply chain-resilient technology solutions company that has a massive AI portfolio.

David is an amazing representative of our brand. He is a wonderful personality for one, but two, he also has an eclectic mix of hobbies. He’s a chicken farmer, small business owner, he builds motorcycles. He embodies what we would consider a potential super-user, and he does use Lenovo. He's very much a brand ambassador of our product line, so when we looked at potential spokespersons for the World Cup, the Beckham-soccer fit was very natural. Lenovo is a global brand. FIFA is a global property, so we did not look at it as a U.S. spokesperson. We looked at it as a global spokesperson.

It's a very different audience. [For the men] we saw massive North America engagement when the North America countries were engaged. We saw engagement stay higher than expectations, drop significantly after the U.S. and Canada were out.

For the women, our U.S. and Canadian teams are just powerhouses. During the men’s tournament, we planned for a lot of our early bespoke, larger events, larger engagements to be really in those early to quarterfinal rounds, and then transitioned to a lot of branding and hospitality as we got into the semis and the finals. For the Women's World Cup, we have to think of it very differently because the eyes of my audience here in North America, I believe, are going to be affixed to this equally across the entire tournament, hopefully all the way into the finals.

For us in North America, the marketing KPIs for the Women's World Cup we feel will exceed what we saw during the men's. That’s for the reasons we just spoke about. Canada and the U.S. women are powerhouses. So, we're really excited to double down in areas that maybe we didn't double down during the men's World Cup.

The men's had more countries, it had more games. There's less [with the Women’s World Cup]. We have a chance to focus our energy, especially our marketing energy, on fewer, bigger campaigns. It'll be everything from bespoke events to hospitality, just being there, to physical sponsorships, to fan fests. There's going to be a couple large fan fests in Rio de Janeiro and Sao Paulo that we’ll have a pretty big presence at.

As far as differentiation in the tech stack, everybody says they're everything to everybody. In reality, from an ecosystem perspective, Lenovo is the only technology provider that can provide everything from phone to PC to all the adjacencies, the cold screens and mice and portables and battery backups, all the way to the most complex data center solutions.

The answer [to standing out] is storytelling. You'll see a lot of tech companies attach themselves to a brand or a sports brand or a property, but only tell that story as it resonates with the sport. We need to tell that story so it resonates beyond the sports where you're just not getting the value that you should get out of what is a very expensive partnership.

#7356. Future of TV Briefing: What it costs brands to fund long-form creator series

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of TV Briefing covers the latest in streaming and TV for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Wednesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

This week’s Future of TV Briefing looks at the economics behind brands sponsoring creator-led long-form original video series.

A creator-led long-form original video series can cost a brand anywhere from $150,000 to $5 million, according to Cyndi Steele, a former TV executive producer who leads talent management firm G&B Digital Management’s &Beyond Creators’ Studio.

A six- to seven-figure investment may be an eye-popping amount for CFOs wary of buying into creators. But paying that premium begins to make more sense as video platforms like YouTube, Instagram and TikTok have started highlighting serialized shows and as brands treat creators less like media properties and more like creative collaborators.

Fifty-seven percent of brands said that generating content is a goal for their creator campaigns, according to a study by LTK and Northwestern University released last month. Your reaction to that stat may be something like, “Isn’t generating content 100% the point of brands hiring creators for campaigns?” Yeah. But the shift underlying the stat is how brands are looking to squeeze more content out of the content that creators produce beyond social.

Recycling creator content to this extent requires a lot of content. Which is where the long-form series pitch can come in.

Consider a brand-sponsored, creator-led series that spans 10 long-form episodes plus three short-form videos per episode for a total of 40 pieces of content. That can average between $300,000 to $500,000, according to Steele. Again, that’s a pretty penny. But consider the cost for a brand to produce a traditional TV show.

“When I was in unscripted reality TV, let’s say 10 episodes of a 40-minute-long show, the budget for that is usually around like $5 million. That’s only to do literally the physical production, nothing else,” Steele said.

There are different levers a brand can pull on to adjust the costs of a creator-led long-form show. The episode count can be changed, for example. But the primary line items include:

IP ownership is the least settled aspect of the deal, as Digiday recently reported. For starters, it’s subject to how many ownership the creator retains as well as &Beyond’s ownership stake. The studio prefers first to agree to an ownership split with the creator.

“If it’s an original concept that we’re developing as the exclusive development and production partner, we do split ownership with our talent. A lot of times we like to have the talent typically have the majority ownership. So it a lot of times looks like a 60-40 or 70-30 [split],” Steele said.

But then where does the brand’s cut come from? Well, that becomes a re-appraisal of everyone’s equity. But only if the brand opts to pay for IP ownership.

“The way we’ve approached it so far is they’re not owning the IP. But we can set up specifically owned assets within there, and they can have first right of refusal for future seasons,” Steele said.

Then if a show succeeds and there are future seasons, the brand can pick back up the IP ownership conversation.

“From a brand’s perspective, I think a lot of them aren’t ready to fully dive in with a full IP investment at that level because it is something that takes a long time to really come back and to grow. But from our standpoint, it’s fine because we approach it [by] pulling a little bit from the model of how our managers are currently doing brand deals with the talent and then also how we experience doing brand integrations from the traditional side. It’s just a lot more flexibility,” said Steele.

“When a federal agency formally recognizes millions of creator-led businesses, the people who decide where money goes start to treat creators as an asset class.”

$1.3 billion: Roku’s platform revenue in Q3 2026.

2-3%: Percentage share of Netflix’s London-based employees who are being laid off.

$2.08 billion: Fox’s advertising revenue in Q3 2026.

$11.2 billion: YouTube’s ad revenue in Q3 2026.

20%: The cost reduction that Disney is aiming for as it plans more layoffs.

$373 million: Versant’s ad revenue in Q3 2026.

#7354. Wendy’s takes a shot at McDonald’s with rewards-based video game

“WendyQuest: Legend of the Four Corner Stones” challenges players to defeat enemies like the Golden Archers, and arrives as the chain chases a turnaround.

Wendy’s is continuing to target gaming communities with the launch of “WendyQuest,” its first fully-owned game. The standalone experience could help Wendy’s capture stronger engagement as the in-game advertising landscape grows saturated: Spending on in-game ads is expected to top $131 billion in 2026 and grow to over $217 billion by 2031.

“WendyQuest: Legend of the Four Corner Stones” involves puzzles with turn-based battles, challenging players to go up against battle mechs, or oversized combat vehicles, including the Mech Chicken — a clear shot at rival McDonald’s. Players can power up with Jr. Bacon Cheeseburgers while navigating five food-focused regions, each of which offers a chance to earn Wendy’s food offers.

The effort offers up to 10 hours of gameplay and was built by hand over several years without the help of artificial intelligence shortcuts, according to the brand. Available prizes include a free medium fry, small frosty, 10 piece nuggets, biscuit or croissant or Dave’s Single. To redeem the reward, consumers need to copy a promo code from the inventory section of the game and enter it into the offers section of the Wendy’s app.

The launch of “WendyQuest” follows other gaming-focused efforts from the chain, including a tabletop role playing game in 2019 called “Feast of Legends,” which challenged players to fight against frozen beef. The chain also activated in Fortnite’s Food Fight game mode.

Other marketers have sought to appeal to gamers, from Panda Express to E.l.f. Cosmetics. Chili’s launched an online video game in 2024 digging at fast food restaurants that have jacked up prices. Gaming represents a valuable opportunity for brands looking to reach a wider audience: two-thirds of Americans between the ages of 5 and 90 game at least one hour per week, according to 2026 data from the Entertainment Software Association.

Wendy’s is currently in the midst of deploying a turnaround plan following ongoing challenges related to traffic and sales growth. The turnaround, which includes a focus on creating distinctive branding and marketing, is being led by Bob Wright, who has served as president and CEO since May. The chain in August brought on ex-McDonald’s CMO Tariq Hassan as chief marketing and customer growth officer. McDonald’s recently unveiled a new strategic plan that sees it shifting its approach to advertising and loyalty.

Wendy’s on Nov. 5 reported its third-quarter earnings, which include a 5.7% decrease in U.S. same-restaurant sales and a 2.2% increase in international same-restaurant sales, according to an earnings release.

#7350. What DoorDash’s Instacart acquisition says about the modern delivery platform pitch

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This story was first published by Digiday sibling Modern Retail.

These days, delivery platforms have to offer more than just delivery services to brands and retailers.

DoorDash’s announced plans to acquire Instacart for $17 billion point to the constant need to gain market share by diversifying services and offerings.

Over the years, both DoorDash and Instacart have invested heavily in new services like AI assistants, advertising programs and in-store B-to-B solutions. DoorDash, in particular, has been on an expansion streak, acquiring U.K.-based competitor Deliveroo and restaurant reservations platform SevenRooms in 2025. Last year, DoorDash also acquired search ad startup Symbiosys for $175 million to deepen its advertising capabilities.

With the high cost of last-mile delivery, companies like DoorDash and Instacart have expanded well beyond delivering groceries and takeout.

Leah Sallen, U.S. head of CPG and retail strategy at programmatic ad tech platform MiQ, said the growing services and tools DoorDash has come to provide have made it more than just a marketplace for delivery. “Between delivery, grocery shopping, restaurant reservations and advertising, DoorDash is gaining access to more consumer touchpoints and insights into when, where and how people shop,” she said.

Over the past few years, DoorDash has aggressively built out its non-restaurant marketplace, partnering with major grocery, convenience and retail brands like Ulta, Skims and Macy’s. The company also introduced logistics features to solve pain points, like Dasher Returns. On the technology side, DoorDash has rolled out AI-powered tools, such as Ask DoorDash for shopping recommendations, DashBuddy for courier support and DashOS for restaurant customer management. These solutions are meant to create an all-in-one ecosystem for B-to-B infrastructure and logistics streamlining.

Now, the Instacart consolidation will bring DoorDash grocery retailers that the platform currently does not offer its customers, notably Walmart and Sam’s Club. But with that market share also comes challenges in maintaining retail relationships by offering more than just delivery solutions.

Neil Saunders, managing director of GlobalData, said DoorDash’s latest hefty acquisition will require further streamlining to make sense as a long-term investment.

Saunders said Instacart’s operations still largely rely on workers manually picking groceries. He said this is not only labor-intensive, but it’s also harder to scale efficiently as digital grocery orders continue to grow. “Either way, DoorDash should aim to position itself as a technology and infrastructure partner rather than simply a delivery provider,” Saunders said.

Some experts believe DoorDash’s proactive strategy is to simply keep up with Amazon’s delivery offerings. However, retail media networks have also become an important revenue source for delivery platforms in recent years.

At its surface, the DoorDash-Instacart deal is about two massive last-mile companies consolidating. But for advertisers, it could mean a broader disruption in retail loyalty, said Sallen, as the two platforms’ merger brings together deep data on restaurant, grocery and retail shopping.

“We have already seen this play out with TikTok Shop, which fundamentally changed consumer shopping behavior by using sophisticated algorithms to deliver highly personalized product recommendations,” Sallen said. A similar shift can happen with this acquisition, Sallen said, thanks to the combination of DoorDash’s immediate-consumption insights and Instacart’s long-standing grocery data.

Sallen explained that, similar to how Amazon became a starting point for shopping, consumers may increasingly turn to platforms like DoorDash to find the best product, price or delivery option before deciding which retailer to purchase from. In turn, she said, “That shift from selecting the retailer first to selecting the retailer last could prompt CPG brands to reconsider how they allocate their retail media dollars.”

Matt Gilbert, CEO of marketing platform Partnerize, agreed that the significance of a DoorDash and Instacart combination goes well beyond delivery.

“It’s about the growing concentration of consumer discovery, advertising, purchasing decisions and transaction data within a single commerce ecosystem,” Gilbert said. For consumers, that could mean greater convenience and fewer steps between discovering a product and buying it. “For advertisers, it creates an opportunity to connect marketing investment more directly to purchasing behavior across more categories and occasions.”

On the other hand, Gilbert said that when platforms increasingly control the consumer experience, the transaction and the reporting of advertising performance, advertisers need assurance that they’re measuring genuine incremental influence — “and not simply taking credit for purchases that would have happened anyway,” said Gilbert.

Sallen added that, as platforms like DoorDash add capabilities and AI increasingly influences shopping decisions, “The consumer path to purchase is becoming more diluted and harder for advertisers to follow.”

That shift could become even more pronounced as AI agents increasingly help consumers weigh price, proximity and product availability before deciding what and where to buy. Sallen said this could mean delivery behemoths like DoorDash could have an advantage in shaping AI-driven recommendations to millions of consumers. “The more intelligence it [DoorDash] can bring together, the more personalized it can make the shopping experience,” Sallen said.

“DoorDash’s growing portfolio of services could position it as a one-stop shop for retailers, with data becoming one of its biggest advantages,” Sallen added.

Ad tech’s winners and losers emerge as CTV growth masks open-web struggles.

The New York Times, People Inc., News Corp and Reuters report revenue growth despite AI disruption, but face tougher comparisons ahead.
