#7323. How TF is the grocery store a media channel?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This WTF guide, sponsored by Grocery TV, explores how marketers are reimagining in-store retail media to engage consumers inside the grocery store.

Even with the growth of digital channels such as e-commerce, social and CTV, the physical store remains one of the most influential moments in the path to purchase.

According to Grocery TV’s 2026 In-Store Shopper Perception Report, 95% of grocery shoppers make at least half of their purchase decisions in-store. Even younger, in-demand audiences frequent physical retail locations, with 55% of Gen Z consumers shopping in-store at least once a week.

Despite this, marketers have historically treated the store more like a retail backdrop than a media channel. Media buyers, especially digital media buyers, have long defaulted to other channels that were easier to tap into. For digital buyers accustomed to unified inventory, real-time optimization and closed-loop attribution, in-store was the opposite: a fragmented, retailer-by-retailer negotiation with no standardized metrics.

Now, in-store can be bought through a single platform, targeted with the precision of digital and measured across the funnel, making it possible to plan against the store with more sophistication.

Investment is following, with eMarketer projecting that U.S. in-store retail media spending will cross the billion-dollar milestone to reach $1.15 billion by 2029.

“We’re seeing advertisers lean in, testing and learning what works now that the infrastructure and measurement capabilities are in place,” said Marlow Nickell, co-founder and CEO of Grocery TV. “There’s a lot of opportunity ahead as more retailers launch their in-store networks.”

In this WTF explainer guide, Digiday and Grocery TV examine how savvy brands, retailers and agencies are leveraging the grocery store as a media channel. The majority of brand choice is made in a retail environment — not on TV or online — and now is the time for nonendemic and CPG marketers alike to rethink how they use the grocery store to engage audiences.

In-store grocery media uses digital screens placed throughout grocery stores to reach shoppers during their shopping trips.

The channel combines the strengths of three other established media channels:  the reach and context of DOOH, the storytelling capabilities of video and the audience and commerce connection of retail media. These qualities come together inside the physical grocery environment, reaching consumers during their routine grocery shopping trip.

Nickell sees proximity to the purchase decision as the key distinction. “With digital out-of-home, it’s often more on-the-go media, like a billboard you’re driving past. In-store retail media is much closer to the point of purchase, where shoppers are making decisions and considering different products,” said Nickell.

Chelsea Monaco, svp, commerce media, Digitas North America, noted that this blending of channels makes classification tricky. “In-store retail media today can be categorized multiple ways, and it varies by the organization,” she said.

“The intersection of digital and in-store blurs in the retail media space, and the investment sources are so unique,” Monaco said. “I’ve typically seen it most categorized as shopper marketing, but investment source and where it sits in the organization depict the true categorization.”

Grocery trips offer more opportunities for product discovery than checking items off a list might suggest. Shoppers can encounter new products, respond to promotions and make unplanned decisions as they browse the aisles.

According to Grocery TV’s In-Store Shopper Perception 2026 report, 32% of shoppers enter the store without a list, and 78% make at least one impulse purchase during their trip.

In-store media can influence those decisions. Grocery TV research found that 62% of shoppers have purchased something directly after seeing it on an in-store screen. Screens are also shoppers’ top way to learn about sales, promotions and new products, allowing brands to directly engage shoppers at the point of purchase.

“When you ask people what they like most about grocery shopping, usually the answer is the discovery. They like the fact that they can see and hold things, and really learn about new products,” Nickell said. “That’s why we find shoppers are open to advertising. They’re already in a buying mindset.”

That opportunity is only growing. In 2026, shopper receptivity increased across every major store zone compared to Grocery TV’s 2023 study. Digital display formats saw an average 10-percentage-point increase in positive customer experience ratings, climbing from 41% to 51%. This suggests that in-store media can be relevant to a broad range of grocery shoppers.

Grocery retailers with in-store media networks run digital screens throughout the store, concentrated in high-traffic and high-dwell spots like entrances and checkout where shoppers pass through or wait. Brands match their creative to the products and audiences they want to reach, such as a financial services brand connecting with shoppers as they wait at checkout.

“The screen is physically there in the environment,” Nickell said. “You don’t have to worry about an ad being blocked or scrolled out of view in the same way you do online.”

Where screens appear also matters. Research from Grocery TV shows that shoppers are more receptive to screens in high-dwell areas, where they naturally spend time waiting, such as the pharmacy, deli and checkout. Shoppers rated these zones as 84% favorable, according to Grocery TV’s 2026 In-Store Shopper Perception Report.

Entrances are another opportunity, as shoppers are accustomed to seeing signage there. By contrast, placements that interrupt the shopping experience, such as cooler screens, tend to be less popular.

Effective in-store creative reflects both the product being advertised and the context in which shoppers encounter it. Brands can tailor messaging to the shopping environment, seasonal needs or specific audiences.

Nickell encourages brands to advertise across the entire store, with creative that connects to the shopping environment and gives shoppers a reason to engage beyond their planned purchases.

For instance, a snack brand might build a campaign around a game-day occasion, using creative that inspires shoppers to add something to their basket whether they see it entering the store or waiting at checkout. Education-forward content, such as recipes or tips for choosing an insurance carrier, can also give shoppers something useful to engage with during their visit.

Shoppers are 2.5 times more likely to consider a brand when an ad is contextually relevant to its environment, according to research from Grocery TV. The surrounding context can also shape how shoppers perceive an ad: 84% viewed end-cap ads featuring contextually relevant creative favorably or neutrally, compared with 79% for ads that were unrelated to the environment.

“As an industry, we often focus on the capability or the creative, but we forget about the overall experience,” Monaco said. “The biggest miss that I’ve seen is not connecting the entire shopper journey or that experience in how that shopper is going to engage with your brand in-store. What experience do you want that shopper to have with your brand at that touchpoint, and how does that fit in with all of the other omnichannel touchpoints they might experience?”

Brands getting started can adapt existing creative from channels such as CTV or social, making adjustments for the in-store environment. Text should be easy to read from a distance, and branding should be prominent. Over time, brands can develop creative specifically for in-store placements, incorporating context such as location, retailer and time of day.

As in-store media has evolved, audience targeting has become more sophisticated, bringing it closer to the strategies marketers already use in TV and digital campaigns. By combining geographic, demographic and behavioral signals with the context of the physical store, advertisers can reach shoppers close to the point of purchase with greater precision.

Geographic targeting can narrow a campaign by state, designated market area (DMA), ZIP code or point of interest. A brand looking to reach students and other campus communities, for example, could target grocery stores near universities.

Behavioral signals, such as purchase patterns and responsiveness to promotions, can help brands refine their audiences further. Demographic signals like age, income and household composition offer another layer. Brands can also prioritize stores that over-index for specific shopper groups, such as Hispanic consumers or older adults.

For example, a telecom brand promoted its international access plan across Grocery TV’s retailer-owned network in stores that over-index for Hispanic shoppers. The campaign drove a 27% lift in consideration for the brand when shopping for a wireless plan, a 15% lift in purchase intent and a 12% lift in unaided brand awareness.

As in-store media becomes easier to plan and buy, marketers have more ways to evaluate its impact, from brand awareness and consideration to incremental sales and repeat purchase. Yet measurement hasn’t always kept pace with the range of outcomes the channel can influence. A 2026 Grocery TV and IAB study found that, across four of five key outcomes, more marketers believe in-store media can drive those outcomes than they can currently measure those outcomes.

Part of the challenge is operational. Brands can struggle to align internal KPIs and standardize measurement across campaigns, while ownership of in-store media still sits largely with shopper marketing teams. The marketers getting the most from in-store take a broader approach, measuring beyond immediate sales and involving national media and brand teams.

Measurement should start with the campaign objective, using tailored methodologies to track different outcomes:

Together, these approaches help advertisers assess performance across the funnel, using methods suited to the outcome they want to understand.

For example, a Grocery TV campaign for a financial health app measured brand impact against a control group, finding an 82% lift in brand rating, a 72% lift in recommendation and a 34% lift in brand awareness. The results show how in-store campaigns can be evaluated against objectives beyond immediate product sales.

“For banking, it makes a lot of sense to run ads at checkout. That’s when you’re pulling cards out of your wallet and making what, for most people, is the largest purchase of the week. Personal finances are already top of mind,” Nickell said.

Sales can be measured, too. In a campaign for Frito-Lay, Hy-Vee’s RedMedia and Grocery TV used a matched-store study across 103 stores, comparing test and control groups over a month. The campaign generated a 15% sales lift and a $7.94 return on ad spend.

Transaction-based measurement can also reveal effects beyond the advertised product. One national personal care brand working with Grocery TV saw a 15% sales lift for the advertised product, a 12% lift across complementary products and an 8% lift across its full brand portfolio.

The opportunity is to measure outcomes aligned with the campaign’s goals, including what happens after the initial purchase. In the IAB study, 75% of marketers who said they fully leverage in-store media measured post-purchase outcomes such as loyalty and repeat purchase, compared with 42% of those who said they underutilize the channel. Measuring beyond immediate sales can give marketers a fuller picture of the role in-store media plays in driving customer retention.

The grocery store can play a complementary role in an integrated plan, connecting brand messaging across channels with the moment shoppers are making purchase decisions. It starts with a clear business objective: What does the campaign need to achieve, and where could in-store contribute?

That may mean reconsidering how budgets are allocated across channels. “The ultimate question is what business outcome will drive the most success,” Monaco said. “We’ve seen cases where it might make sense to take budget out of underperforming digital and move it to in-store.”

A study from Grocery TV and MFour found that in-store media delivers an average of 49% incremental reach beyond existing media plans. Complementing campaigns on other channels with in-store media allows marketers to reduce overexposure by diversifying audiences. They can also maximize media dollars by driving new engagement and closing gaps left by other channels, such as display and online video.

Marketers don’t need to rethink their entire media mix to explore the channel, however. They can start with a defined objective, test in-store alongside other media and evaluate its incremental contribution to outcomes like brand lift or sales. Those findings can help determine where future investment will have the greatest impact.

In-store retail media is still developing as a full-funnel media-buying channel, giving early adopters an edge before it becomes a standard part of media planning. As Nickell noted, brands that start testing now can learn which creative works, how to measure success and how to make the case for investment internally. Those learnings take time to build. “The earlier you get started, the better,” Nickell said.

Ad tech’s winners and losers emerge as CTV growth masks open-web struggles.

#7322. L’Oréal’s evolving microdrama strategy puts entertainment over product

A ReelShort series from Urban Decay themed around ghosting dates generated 3.8 million views in five days.

L’Oréal was early to the branded microdrama boom in the U.S., running a holiday effort for Maybelline last year that riffed on Hallmark romance movie tropes. The beauty and cosmetics giant around Halloween again dipped its toe into the short-form video genre that has ballooned in popularity as marketers produce entertainment they hope can attract scroll-oriented viewers but requires a different touch than a glossy ad campaign.

The company’s Urban Decay brand on Oct. 24 debuted a vertical video series on ReelShort titled “Ghost Hunter Hotline,” which runs six episodes that generally last just a few minutes each. The story follows protagonist Zoe as she dishes out justice to romantic interests who suddenly went silent on the communications front, or “ghosted” her and her friends, while showing off Urban Decay’s aptly named Ghosting Spray product. A former fling eventually returns and throws a wrench in some of Zoe’s plans, the type of soapy twist typical to cliffhanger-heavy microdramas. The overarching idea was to blend elements typical to the spooky season with tongue-in-cheek commentary on modern dating culture.

“Ghost Hunter Hotline” generated 3.8 million views in the five-day window after launch while sitting at No. 2 on ReelShort’s most-viewed shorts ranking, according to data shared by the brand. L’Oréal’s microdrama experiments are part of a larger effort to think differently from an old-school advertising mindset that puts the chief focus on product messaging and brand story.

“It’s something that we’ve now known in the creator era for the last couple of years, which is that you really need to think about your audience first, and you think about entertainment first as the primary purpose [of marketing],” said Han Wen, chief digital and marketing officer at L’Oréal USA.

Rather than create the microdrama content with an external agency, as it had done in the past, L’Oréal produced, developed and distributed “Ghost Hunter Hotline” in collaboration with ReelShort, relying on its knowledge of what appeals to the genre’s audience. ReelShort, which is owned by Crazy Maple Studio, is one of the leading dedicated microdrama platforms, on track to generate over $1 billion in revenue this year, according to third-party industry analyses.

“We went into it knowing that we wanted to test directly with a platform rather than a more hybrid format,” said Wen.

Urban Decay proved to be the right fit for the content type in a L’Oréal portfolio that spans dozens of brands. Halloween is a major occasion for the luxury label, which was founded in the mid-’90s in Los Angeles and promotes an edgier, antibland image. L’Oréal acquired Urban Decay in 2012 to reach higher-end young shoppers.

Other marketers are turning to microdramas because they can be turned around quickly and on the cheap. “Ghost Hunter Hotline” came together faster than a traditional ad campaign, in about four weeks, according to Wen.

“Speed is really important in the current context of marketing, where trends move very quickly,” said Wen.

Wen acknowledged that not all of L’Oréal’s bets on emerging social video spaces have panned out in the past. She pointed to Periscope, a now-defunct livestreaming service under Twitter, and BeReal, a pandemic-era flash in the pan. Still, Wen expressed optimism about microdramas, which have become a mainstream fixture in China and continue to see traction in the U.S., where mobile-first viewing is becoming more dominant.

“You realize that there is something here that people do engage with organically,” said Wen of microdramas.

“To me, it seems like it's worth learning in terms of how U.S. audiences — and then consumers of our brands — would adapt to that behavior here and make sure that we're on the forefront of that,” Wen added. “The only way that we know that is to actually engage with it and try a couple of different things.”

#7319. Digiday Media Buying Summit October Recap: Assessing the ups and challenges of AI’s spread across the media agency landscape

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of Digiday’s coverage of its Digiday Media Buying Summit. More from the series →

Artificial intelligence has in short order swept across all facets of the media agency world, from internal tools that save time and improve efficiency to planning and measurement tools that dig deeper into oceans of data to identify audiences for their clients, to even creative production that works at geometric levels of produtivity.

But agencies are also called upon to offer consultative guidance to clients on how the latter can put AI to use. The crush of effort has resulted in new priorities and pressures at both independent and holding company.

Each Digiday event kicks off with the edit team breaking down current industry themes. Here’s where we think we are:

AI is inevitable in its influence but also its impact. Whether it’s agentic buying or planning, AEO/GEO or guiding clients through ways to understand and implement AI, it’s changing processes and compressing timelines.

But warning signs are everywhere, from ensuring human oversight of anything to do with client budgets to reining in token usage or insufficient ROI. AI will factor in some way with everything we discuss these next few days.

The drive toward lower funnel, performance results has dragged a spotlight onto the hidden fees that agencies often struggle with or sweep under the rug. How can transparency sit alongside better results?

There’s no stopping this train, as more and more brands opt to devote a larger share of marketing dollars to the field. When is it too much? And how to ensure performance is still delivered.

If you’re a holding company agency, principal media is a reality in some part of your business. But are there ways to be part of it without feeling you sold your soul?

Case for and against: principal mediaDMBS kicked off with an interactive session that invited a debate on principal media, then asked the audience to vote on which side won the debate — since it’s the most widely debated topic in the media agency world. Principal media puts agencies in the position of buying and selling inventory for and to their clients. To its detractors, including Kamran Asghar, CEO of Crossmedia, who argued against, it has corrupted the meaning of “agency” by putting agencies on both sides of the investment equation.

But the argument can be made — as it was by Forrester’s vp and senior agency analyst Jay Pattisall — that it’s here to stay for those agencies with the scale to pull it off, so long as there can be sufficient transparency for the client in the process. The final vote: 72% opposed principal media.

The new agency talent equationAI and emerging tools are making it possible to build ideas that would have been impossible just a few years ago. M+C Saatchi CEO Nadja Bellan-White explored what this convergence means for agency leaders — from how creative and media teams need to work together to the new kinds of talent, skills and perspectives agencies need to hire to meet the demands of clients today. The key: balancing human talent and the temptation of technology, most acutely AI.

Injecting more transparency and certainty in mediaIn modern media investment, too many brands are playing roulette — relying on blind platform algorithms, chasing self-reported metrics and reacting to phantom signals that lead straight to knee-jerk budget pivots. Tack on ecosystems cluttered with opacity, hidden fees and black-box attribution. Feels like the deck’s stacked against media specialists, right? Devin Bellamy of sports gaming disruptor Underdog and Eden Collective founder and CEO Alison Monk engaged in a no-BS look at the radical media rigor and intellectual humility needed to outsmart outspenders.

The creator authenticity problemCreators have become a powerful part of the media mix, but as the business matures, authenticity is getting harder to define — and even harder to protect. A session featuring Alex Mathieu head of creative strategy & vp at creator agency Open Influence, explored what makes creator partnerships actually feel credible, where brands and agencies get it wrong and how marketers can balance scale, performance and genuine audience connection. As Mathieu said:

When media meets creative techThe walls between media, creative, data and technology are getting more porous by the day, especially as AI cuts across them all by dint of its value. How far into the creative and design process should media agencies go? And which AI tools and platforms are actually helping agencies plan, build and buy more effectively, rather than simply adding another layer of tech? Laurel Burton, CEO of Instrument and Carly Gomez, Crocs’ CMO, walked the audience through their website, social media and e-commerce efforts this year, and how technology sped up the process while digging deeper insights.

Retail media’s shakeoutThe retail media gold rush created an increasingly crowded market. Now the question is who survives. Are advertisers finally ready to cull the long tail of lower-value networks, and what separates the retail media businesses that become essential from those that become expendable? Dentsu’s new head of commerce Aisha Khan addressed these issues, concluding that while some consolidation is inevitable, growth in this sector will continue to grow because it offers media agencies new opportunities to harness closed-loop measurement in a way that benefits both upper- and lower-funnel marketing efforts. Khan also believes in-store will be the next area that’s ripe for growth and expansion:

Do you know what AI is costing your agency?

Agencies and clients across the industry are realizing finally that AI gains mean little if you can’t fathom the real operating costs associated with AI systems or agentic tools. Christina Riccitelli, chief innovation & analytics officer, OpAd Media, and Tony Russo, vp AI innovation & strategy, Mile Marker, represented two agencies with different philosophies on the tech, as they attempt to balance the benefits of AI with a close understanding of its actual cost.

Redesigning your teams around AIAgencies across the globe are investing in AI applications in pursuit of productivity gains. Few have thought hard about how to bed in their new capabilities and operationalize the tech. Gila Wilensky, evp and head of data and media, North America at Monks, walked the audience through how the company has rethought staff structures and client-facing teams to fully take advantage of AI:

Go Fish’s approach to agentic planning and buyingAgentic media planning and buying tools have become an area of intense focus for many agencies and clients. Beyond the hype, however, there are decisions to be made over what agents are actually best placed to do, and how much control they require to operate properly. David Dweck, president of Go Fish Digital, called out the opportunities to apply agentic tools while also pointing to areas where it should be kept away:

Making outcomes-based relationships workOutcome or performance-based client relationships have emerged as a response to pressures facing the agency business model. But while the idea can be theoretically attractive, hashing out an outcome-based arrangement with a client can be much harder in practice. Morgan Scarnato, chief strategy officer of CourtAvenue Media, broke down how to construct an outcomes-based relationship that works for both agency and client:

“I tell our staff, ‘Do your resume every six months, and if you’re not learning stuff here, advancing your skill sets, you should quit and go work someplace else.’”

“I’ve actually been surprised by how much AI content [there is at] a higher strategy level … places like the VPs and the C-suite. I know they know how to do these things. I don’t think they want to take the time to do it. Because this is much easier.”

“It comes down to the brief. I’ve seen some of the worst briefs I’ve ever seen in my career recently, and it’s because they’re so disjointed and there’s no through line on what the not only the marketing goal is as well as the business goal … I think AI is making it worse.”

“Yeah, briefs have definitely gotten worse, and they’re also like jam packed with a lot of things that feel like they start to blur together the swim lanes between what media’s job is versus a creative job or a PR driven job.”

“There’s two forks in the road for the world of agencies. One fork is, ‘I’m going to fully automate everything and eliminate as many humans and costs as possible.’ The other fork in the road is, ‘I’m going to create efficiency and enable the humans I already have, and lean into expertise and make those experts more efficient at what they do, so they can deliver more value for clients.’ So, if I was working at an organization that’s going for full automation and limiting humans, I would be scared. I would argue that’s not the right strategy.”

“So if we’re not pushing the staff to learn new things and keep the sort of ship afloat, you’re going to have these issues where an AI bot spends $10 million versus $10,000 in a DSP, and it’s going to be like, ‘I don’t know what to do’.”

AI remains a real question mark on the future of media and marketing. There are so many ways it can help — and agencies are putting it to work. But there are risks for companies cultivating an over-reliance on this technology, from corporate slop to the expanding sea of sameness.

Bottom line: Humans need to remain at the center to make gains and prevent potentially catastrophic mistakes.

Clients can be great partners but that relationship is under pressure to the point of counterproductive behavior and not speaking the same language. That’s causing undue frustration and putting pressure on agencies trying to divine what clients want and need. AI is making that worse.

Bottom line: Find paths to better and more open communication.

In the age of principal media, transparency is the key to success, but a lack of it is getting in the way of agency/client trust. Pressure to make money at holdcos is impacting the way media and marketing is unfolding, and it affects all parties.

Bottom line: Remove black boxes from the equation.

AI implementation is altering the day-to-day work of agency staffers. It’s an open field for agencies reimagine what talent means as the industry transforms at the speed of AI. How you staff up is for grabs.

Bottom line: There are no clear answers today how that is accomplished, but experimentation and testing will yield answers.

There are myriad definitions of outcome-based agency solutions, but more clarity around identity and intent will determine where outcomes land in service to client goals.

Bottom line: Get on the same page with clients on determining the definition and purpose of outcomes.

#7318. GRC Analyst at Supabase

We’re looking for a GRC Analyst to join our Compliance & Security team and help make risk management, audit and compliance a continuous part of how Supabase runs. You’ll work closely with engineering, security, IT, and legal to help us define our risk tolerance, keep our compliance programs (including SOC 2 and ISO/IEC 27001:2022) current, test controls on a rolling basis, and answer customer and vendor security reviews quickly and accurately.

This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions.

Run risk assessments across the company and major product changes. Score likelihood and impact, propose treatments, and bring the results to leadership to inform risk tolerance decisions.

Maintain the risk register, track remediation to closure, and report status to compliance and security leadership.

Monitor controls on a rolling basis, using automated tests where they exist and manual testing where they don’t. Triage failures, prioritize by the risk register, and align each finding with an owner and a due date.

Support internal audit, whether performed by an external partner or in-house: propose scope from the risk register, supply evidence, and track findings to closure.

Run day-to-day operations for our compliance programs: evidence collection, control owner follow-up, and readiness ahead of each audit period.

Extend continuous control validation beyond what our compliance platform covers. Build custom tests with AI coding tools, work with engineering and IT to pull evidence from source systems instead of collecting screenshots, and check that each test proves what the control requires.

Have 3+ years of experience in GRC, internal audit, or security compliance at a SaaS or technology company

Are comfortable working with SOC 2 and ISO 27001 controls, a compliance platform such as Drata, Vanta, and evidence from systems like AWS, GCP, Google Workspace, and Okta

Communicate clearly across both technical and non-technical audiences

Have experience in async or globally distributed teams

Are energized by solving real-world problems for developers

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.

Location independence is the future of work. It serves companies with best talent from around the globe. And benefits employees with a greater work life balance and the ability to work in their pyjamas.- Nithur, founder.

#7317. McDonald’s US CMO steps down as sales decline

As the fast-food giant searches for a new marketing leader, it will split the role between two longtime executives on an interim basis.

Alyssa Buetikofer, chief marketing and customer experience officer of McDonald’s USA, will leave the company next month to spend more time with her family, according to an internal note obtained by Marketing Dive. A 15-year veteran at McDonald’s, the executive was appointed to her latest role less than two years ago when Tariq Hassan stepped down.

As the company conducts a search for its new U.S. CMO, its marketing will be supported by two current executives in interim roles. Global CMO and Head of New Business Ventures Morgan Flatley will also serve as interim chief creative officer, while company veteran Charlie Newberger will serve as interim senior vice president, U.S. chief marketing and commercial officer.

The shakeup arrives a day after McDonald’s reported that U.S. comparable sales declined 0.2% in Q3 2026. However, global comparable sales increased 1.5% and the company beat Wall Street expectations.

Buetikofer will remain with McDonald’s through Dec. 18 to help support the leadership transition. The note cited her impact on McDonald’s business, where she recently oversaw the return of Snack Wraps and Monopoly, as well as the launch of a new beverage platform and a successful Grinch collaboration.

Flatley previously served as U.S. chief marketing and digital customer experience officer from 2017 to 2021 before taking on the global leadership role. She is described in the note as the driving force behind the brand’s “Fan Truths” strategy and the “Famous Orders” platform.

“In today’s environment, creativity is the difference maker for brands, and Morgan’s leadership puts us in a strong position to develop big ideas that build the brand and accelerate growth,” according to the note.

Newberger has been at McDonald’s for more than 17 years and was most recently corporate vice president and global category head for beverages and desserts. In the interim, he will lead marketing operations and work to translate strategy into commercial execution across the U.S. business.

The leadership moves and CMO search come as McDonald’s works to implement a strategic plan that it unveiled in June and detailed at a September investor day. Marketing is part of a consumer-focused pillar of the plan that includes creating brand-led content and co-creating with fans to reach consumers in relevant and personalized ways. McDonald’s last month also stepped away from the FIFA World Cup, a move that could disrupt the QSR sports sponsorship space.

#7314. Future of Marketing Briefing: Creators had a big year. Christmas still went to the celebrities

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Creators are everywhere in marketing these days — everywhere except Christmas ads.

The first batch of festive campaigns, from John Lewis, SharkNinja, Ulta Beauty and Burberry among others, makes little for the industry’s favorite obsession. Despite leaning on creators throughout the rest of the year, marketers aren’t doing it for the holiday crunch. That job still belongs to celebrities.

Sephora has cast Seth Rogen as a man hopelessly out of his depth, rescued by one of the retailer’s Beauty Advisors before he can do any lasting damage. Over at Kay, Saturday Night Live’s Colin Jost is put through that most well-worn of Christmas tropes: the frantic hunt for the perfect present for someone special. Sports Direct roped in Nike to push the cliche a step further. Its ad follows an uncle wandering the aisles in search of something for his niece, blissfully unaware that a squad of Nike athletes is quietly steering him to the right choice. And Gucci has Mariah Carey — the industry version of the relative who gets passed from house to house every December. This year it’s the luxury brand’s turn to host her.

Why these brands do this year after year is no secret. Celebrities, particularly the calibre fronting this year’s crop, are the advertising equivalent of tinsel: nobody calls it innovative but a tree looks bare without it. For CMOs wary of how quickly an ad can set off the internet these days, that familiarity is a safety blanket. John Lewis is the latest case. Within a day of release, its spot had Piers Morgan calling it “possibly the worst advert I’ve ever seen for anything” and Mumsnet testing into the “ungrateful” grandchildren. Dame Kristin Scott Thomas, the ad’s star, escaped without a scratch.

“Part of the reason brands use celebrities in ads is because they’re borrowing fame,” said Lynne Deason, head of creative excellence at Kantar. “Christmas is this mass market moment where brands are trying to reach everyone and so often brands are looking for a celebrity that everyone will recognize rather than maybe a particular audience.”

None of which is a knock on creators. Indeed, marketers have spent the year handing creators budget, stage time and, increasingly, the lead role in global campaigns. Christmas ads, however, play by older rules.  It is the most scrutinized piece of work a brand releases all year, with the biggest budget and the most senior sign-off, and its conventions have barely changed in a decade: a big film, a broad audience and, more often than not, a famous face. Striking to that formula keeps a brand in line with every rival in the ad break. Leaving it puts the brand out on its own and few CMOs want to be the one doing that in December.

“That’s perhaps harder with creators because people tend to tap into them for particular interests,” said Deason. “Obviously there are some who are becoming very famous in their own right with mass audiences but there are fewer of those. And Chrismtas is such a competitive time.”

How long that takes is anyone’s guess. Plenty of creators are household names within their own niche but Christmas needs a face that granny and the teenagers on the sofa will bith recognise. Take Tesco’s Christmas ad starring comedian Roisin Conaty and actor Anna Maxwell Martin. It is currently the most talked about commercial in the U.K, according to Sprout Social. So far, people have posted or talked about the ad 3,263 times (volume) racking up over 36,000 total interactions like likes, comments and shares (engagement). Attitude toward it, however, is divided, with only 51% of conversations positive. This is due, according to the researchers, to people feeling that the advert’s premise is unrealistic and presents a lack of awareness over how retail works. Even so, two famous actors got the country talking, which is more than most festive ads manage.

“A CMO is always looking for ideas they couldn’t get inside the company,” said Linda Boff, former CMO of GE and CEO of independent marketing services group Said Differently. “Fresh, bold ideas that zag when everyone else zigs. Having been on that side of the table, I know the pressure to deliver results, especially during the holidays. But when everyone follows the same formula, playing it safe can actually be the bigger risk. The best ideas are rooted in a brand’s DNA, and that’s something I always encourage our team to push for.”

It’s also why the next few years are likely to bring festive campaigns that put stars and creators side by side. The biggest creators now pull audiences that many TV names would envy and plenty of celebrities have learned to behave like creators – filming their own content and talking straight to fans on their own feeds. SharkNina’s holiday campaign shows how this already works. Kevin Hart, David Beckham, Aryna Siarhiejeŭna and Tom Bradyset up the story by posting short teasers to their social accounts days before the actual spot landed. Laura Dyer, senior vp of global brand and partnerships at SharkNinja, calls the line between the two groups “super fluid.” Before long, the lead in a Christmas ad could to whoever has the most devoted following, whether they made their name on TV, film or a smartphone screen.

Marc Jacobs seasonal ad is a case in point. Comedian Megan Stalter has been invited to a party at a downturn hotel by Interview editor-in-chief Mell Ottenburg but nobody told her the room number. So she starts cold-calling roos and roping in the bellhop, and bumps into a cast that looks a lot like the future of marketing over this period. New York Knicks center Andre Drummond, social media personality Ashtin Earle, model and actress Kai Schreiber alongside designer Miyako Bellizzi, and models Mathilda Gvarliani and Amina Ahmed. Earle gets the same billing as the other celebrities around her.

“Brands know creators have influence, but they’re still figuring out how to make the most of those relationships,” said Bodd, whose agencies developed the campaign for Marc Jacobs. “And as the creator economy continues to grow, we’ll see brands become more thoughtful about who they work with and why.

Anthropologie certainly has. The retailer handed its holiday campaign to the comedian and creator of the hit viral web series Brooklyn Coffee Shop Pooja Tripathi, who wrote and showran a micro comedy series about a holiday party “from script to shoot”, according to CMO Barbra Sainsurin. The brand stuck to what it knows best, dressing the cast and styling the set. The rollout has trailers and teasers “like a movie launch” and about 50 more creators will spread it across Instagram, TikTok, YouTube, Pinterest and Reddit. The plan, said Sainsurin, is to keep deciding dstory by story which ones go to creators and which stay with the brand.

“We’re using the holiday to challenge ourselves beyond just the traditional way in which we tell stories and to create a more immersive brand experience through this extension and partnership with an original content creator.”

Kelsey Gray Sherman spent years at WME brokering deals between celebrities and the brands that want them. Last month Lego poached her. She takes up a newly created role as the toymaker’s head of celebrity talent and brand-first partnerships and reports to Jill Wilfert, svp of entertainment, partnerships and content. Lego already knows its way around a box office, having pulled in nearly $700 million from two Lego Movies. A company with that track record hiring an agency dealmaker suggests Lego wants more of its entertainment built around its own brand with famous names and outside partners brought in to amplify.

Over email, Gray Sherman explained what she has planned for the role.

The LEGO Group has a long history of iconic collaborations across its business – from product to entertainment, gaming and more. As those partnerships have grown in recent years, so has the need for dedicated in-house expertise and coordination, which is where this new position was created.

My role will be to connect those dots across a business of talented people already doing fantastic work, and to create a consistent approach to engaging talent while identifying strategic opportunities to extend and elevate relationships across multiple touch points.

What does it say about where you see the future of celebrity talent and brand first partnerships headed? For instance, does celebrity talent include creators now?

The LEGO Group’s invitation to its partners is simple: come create something with us that will help inspire and develop the builders of tomorrow. Looking ahead, the LEGO brand is excited to build and explore new relationships building on this mission, in a way that can extend across different parts of the business and evolve over time.

That absolutely includes creators. The distinction between “traditional celebrity” and creator is increasingly less meaningful to audiences, so the brand’s approach to building and executing relationships with all manner of talent reflects that.

The focus of my role will be on represented talent, while we have several in-house teams that focus specifically on fan creators and local influencers. In collaboration with the brand’s best-in-class social and influencer teams, my role will help bring consistency to those relationships while maximizing the impact and mutual value of each partnership.

How will your  background at WME help here?

My experience across entertainment marketing – and specifically helping grow WME’s brand representation business – brings an expansive perspective on how global brands are engaging top talent and entertainment partners to make an impact in culture.

Beyond providing direct relationships and connectivity, I will be working to help implement best-in-class partnership practices across the organization and streamline how the brand is showing up to industry partners.

What does “work with brands in ways that go beyond traditional licensing and product collaborations” mean for Lego?

It means that product does not always need to be the first point of entry when exploring a partnership. The LEGO Group has an extraordinary history of product innovation with partners, and embracing “brand first partnerships” is not about moving away from that – it is about recognizing the expanding ways two partners can create mutual value for each other and stand-out experiences for fans – with or without product.

“We just need to translate that progress in terms of performance into broader adoption and a larger share of advertiser budgets. I do think AI is a meaningful accelerant there. So as we look into 2027 and beyond, we’re continuing to improve performance with better AI-powered recommendations, and we’re making it easier for advertisers to measure those results using the tools they already rely on, which we know is really important to their confidence in putting more budget behind Snapchat.”

— Snap CEO Evan Spiegel during the company’s Q3 earnings call, when talking about the challenge of turning improvements in ad performance into a larger share of advertiser budgets.

Why AI is boosting Meta’s ad business while hurting Google search

Meta’s ad revenue grew 26% last quarter, more than twice as fast as Google Search’s 12%, as AI tools sharpen Meta’s targeting while chatbots like ChatGPT, Claude and Muse threaten Google’s link-clicking model, per CNBC. Both are spending heavily on AI, but Google is far more diversified, and analysts say its stock won’t recover until it proves it can make money from AI-era search.

Meta Hires Rich Latour to Lead In-House Content Studio

Meta has hired former BlackRock content chief and NBC Nightly News veteran Rich Latour to run its in-house content studio, telling the company’s own story through brand, executive and product content as it pushes into AI and smart glasses, according to The Hollywood Reporter.

Apple’s Services growth is slowing as App Store revenue stalls under legal and gaming pressures, and the author argues advertising, not commissions, is its best way to monetize the App Store’s nearly one billion weekly users, per Mobile Dev Memo.

The number of brands advertising on ChatGPT has climbed past 6,000, but paid ChatGPT traffic converts at about a third of Google’s rate, so OpenAI still has to prove its ads actually work.

WPP says Apple’s Safari crackdown on identity-matching firms has barely affected it, because its InfoSum platform works outside the browser, which strengthens the case for a deal many in the industry once doubted.

Good ads have a new name, ‘entertainment-first’ and SharkNinja is using it for its holiday campaign

SharkNinja’s in-house holiday ad has Kevin Hart, Tom Brady and Aryna Sabalenka wrecking David Beckham’s house, teased with fake CCTV leaks, as the brand bets on “entertainment-first” ads people actually want to share, while still slipping in product demos to drive sales.

Agencies and marketers now frequently use AI tools to create media and creative briefs. But the practice could be a canary in the coalmine for AI overuse in advertising.

The DoorDash-Instacart deal could accelerate commerce media consolidation, promising scale but risking higher prices and lock-in.

#7312. Campaign Trail: Wet Ones puts ‘hand butts’ on the streets

The Edgewell brand worked with BBH USA on an out-of-home campaign it hoped would cause some consumers to do a double take.

It’s cold and flu season, which means it's prime time for consumers to reach for antibacterial hand wipes. And while Wet Ones has been keeping hands clean consistently over the last half century, the Edgewell Personal Care brand had not released a major campaign in years.

Wet Ones decided to change that, seeking to create something fresh, bold and different that could capture the attention of a younger audience. The brand teamed with BBH USA and kept the brief loose: get the brand back out there and in conversations. The result, “Just For Hands,” launched last month and grew from a simple insight: More than 50% of consumers have used hand wipes on an intimate area, according to research commissioned by the brand.

“It was a bit surprising to us because it wasn't something that we had necessarily seen before,” said Nicole Harris, head of marketing for sun and skin at Edgewell. “We can do something with this. This is funny. How can we play on this?”

The insight about how consumers were using hand wipes inspired early rounds of work that played off inanimate objects like peaches. But BBH kept pushing and landed on an idea that the team calls “hand butts:” extreme close-ups of fingers that look like posteriors.

“We wanted to create a double take – that split second when you think you're seeing one thing, and then realize you're looking at something completely different,” said Yohan Daver, executive creative director at BBH USA.

“The craft had to make the illusion feel completely real, and by taking the idea across New York City at scale, we're turning one clever visual device into a much bigger cultural moment,” Daver added.

Created with photographer Ale Burset, a winner of more than 60 Cannes Lions awards, the campaign had a comprehensive out-of-home media plan including LinkNYC kiosks, transit shelters, digital newsstands, wild postings, bus wrapping and a Time Square takeover. Plus, interactive walls dispensed travel packs and individual wipes to pedestrians.

Originally, Wet Ones’ media plan included ads throughout the New York City subway system, where the variety of handheld surfaces like railings and straps provide a “torture test” for the product, Harris explained. That changed when the Metropolitan Transportation Authority vetoed the “Just For Hands” creative.

“When they refused us for being a little too risque, it was perfect,” Harris said. “It added juiciness to the story, but it also forced us to think quickly in terms of the other touch points. How do we want to evolve the media plan to still have the impact, but also take advantage of this from a storytelling perspective?”

Wet Ones’ PR team used the rejection as an angle to drive earned media. The brand also partnered with Kyle Cooke, of reality TV series “Summer House,” for a social stunt that brought a hand butt to celebrity gossip account DeuxMoi. Then, the “Just For Hands” campaign became the target of some friendly banter from Goodwipes, a brand of flushable wipes — not hand wipes — that isn’t a Wet Ones competitor.

“We [wanted] to get a response up in Times Square, so that was probably a record that I've seen, in my tenure for the company, for us to turn around a piece of creative and get it up within hours,” Harris said. That type of speed and agility was central to the “Just For Hands” campaign.

“We kicked it off at the end of August, so really fast turn out of the gate, but [it] led to amazing work,” Harris said. “We're getting great consumer response, but our retailers were really excited about it, too.”

#7311. By the numbers: Ad tech’s widening divide, as AppLovin surges and rivals struggle in Q3

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The latest quarterly earnings from six of the publicly traded ad tech companies reveal a tale of two cohorts, with AppLovin and Unity’s substantial growth contrasting with declining revenues at Criteo, Taboola, and Teads.

The companies, which reported their third-quarter results this week, generated markedly different outcomes during the reported period. AppLovin’s revenue increased 55% to $2.19 billion, while Unity posted 32% growth and Magnite reported a double-digit increase. Meanwhile, three of the six experienced annual revenue declines, with Teads suffering the steepest contraction at 26%.

The results also show differences in profitability, with some companies increasing earnings despite lower revenue. Meanwhile, connected TV remained a source of growth for Magnite and Teads, although it was insufficient to offset the latter’s broader decline.

Overall, the numbers illustrate how unevenly the industry’s growth opportunities are being distributed.

A top-line observation is that the two strongest performers are mobile advertising rivals AppLovin and Unity, for some, a trend that underlines the decline of the open web and the resilience of app-based media — both mobile and CTV — companies.

AppLovin reported $2.19 billion in third-quarter revenue, up 55% year over year, alongside net income of $1.44 billion, an increase of 72%.

The company also forecast Q4 revenue of between $2.365 billion and $2.395 billion, and told analysts that its Q3 revenue exceeded the combined revenue of the other five companies, which totaled about $1.94 billion.

Unity, a mobile advertising rival of AppLovin — the two are involved in a recently filed, ongoing legal dispute — reported revenue of $622 million, up 32%, with the Grow Solutions division, i.e., the division that hosts its media offering, generating $457 million, an increase of 44%. The company also reported that its Vector ad platform grew 126% year over year and 40% sequentially during the reporting period.

Elsewhere, in the cohort, growth was more modest, if not outright in marked decline (see chart below).

Magnite reported revenue of $204.2 million, up 14%, with growth primarily driven by its connected TV business. Meanwhile, Criteo reported Q3 revenue of $416 million, down 11%, while contribution ex-TAC, i.e., after “traffic acquisition costs”, declined 17% to $240 million.

Criteo’s retail media revenue fell 23%, partly reflecting changes involving two (unnamed) clients that reduced business by $20 million, according to executives there.

Meanwhile, Taboola reported revenue of $462.3 million, down 6.9%, amid declining advertising impression volumes. However, the company’s gross profit rose 9.6% to $152.3 million despite the 6.9% revenue decline.

By contrast, Teads (Taboola’s historic rival and one-time prospective merger partner) recorded the largest decline, with revenue falling 26% to $235.1 million. After TAC, its gross profit declined 28% to $93.5 million, according to the filing, with execs there citing continued weakness in open-internet advertising and not reinstating its financial guidance. In total, this equated to a net loss of $325.5 million, compared with a $19.7 million loss a year earlier.

Revenue growth did not consistently align with profitability changes across the six companies in this cohort, with Unity reporting its first profitable quarter since its IPO (see chart below).

By contrast, Teads (Taboola’s historic rival, and one-time prospective merger partner) recorded the largest decline, with revenue falling 26% to $235.1 million. After TAC, its gross profit declined 28% to $93.5 million, according to the filing, with execs there citing continued weakness in open-internet advertising and not reinstating its financial guidance. In total, this equated to a net loss of $325.5 million, compared with a $19.7 million loss a year earlier.

Revenue growth did not consistently correspond with changes in profitability across the six companies in this cohort, with Unity’s fortunes contrasting to Criteo – the company that was initially renowned as an ad-retargeting company, but now preferring to underline its retail media wares – reporting a deterioration in both revenue and profitability. Criteo’s gross margin declined from 55% to 50%, while net income fell 85% to $6 million. The figures show that companies reporting similar revenue trends are experiencing different outcomes in terms of gross profit, net income and cash generation.

For all the (inevitable) talk of AI, CTV remained a significant source of growth for Magnite and Teads during the quarter.

Magnite reported a 39% increase in CTV contribution, ex-TAC, to $105.4 million, compared with 5% growth in its DV+ business, which includes display and other digital video advertising.

CTV accounted for approximately 53% of Magnite’s total contribution, ex-TAC, of $200.6 million, compared with 45% a year earlier. Overall revenue increased 14% to $204.2 million, while total contribution ex-TAC grew 20%.

CTV growth also proved an oasis for Outbrain’s executive team, with revenue from the channel increasing 37% year over year. However, this was offset by the fact that CTV represented just 12% of the company’s quarterly revenue, compared with its overall revenue decline of 26%. Teads reported nearly $100 million in year-to-date CTV revenue.

Taken in tandem, these results show that CTV continues to grow despite weaker performance in other areas of digital advertising. However, the contribution of that growth to overall company performance depends on the CTV business’s size relative to other revenue streams.

The filings mark the midway point in this quarter’s earnings season, with Zeta Global, The Trade Desk, PubMatic and Lifoff Mobile all due to make their equivalent filings next week.

Instagram wants to pull long-form creators away from YouTube, but is it offering enough? Experts weigh in on the platform’s pivot.

The New York Times, People Inc., News Corp and Reuters report revenue growth despite AI disruption, but face tougher comparisons ahead.

DoorDash’s announced plans to acquire Instacart for $17 billion point to the constant need to gain market share by diversifying services and offerings.

#7309. Software Engineer - Turso at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer to join our Turso core database team and help bring forward the future of agentic databases. Turso is both an Open Source database that replaces SQLite—with an ongoing project to also support the Postgres interface—and a Cloud offering where users can create databases on demand. You’ll work closely with customers and other teams at Supabase to build the future of what databases look like in a world where users and agents are creating millions of databases a day.

This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments and is excited about building database technology tools that scale to billions.

Driving the progress of the Turso Open Source database, in particular the Postgres compatibility layer.

Making sure code works, and never breaks, and putting the systems and guarantees in place for that to happen.

Maintaining the Turso Cloud operations, including the Cloud API and data plane operations.

Improving the scalability of the Turso Cloud operations to support a 100x larger number of databases

Have written databases before, and/or other high-stakes low-level systems like Hypervisors, OS Kernels, storage devices, etc.

Have experience working with remote teams and Open Source communities.

Have a passion and experience for correct systems that work, and have the experience in the tools to make that happen: Deterministic Simulation Testing, formal verification methods, fuzz and differential testing, etc.

Have experience deploying, operating and debugging large scale software systems in production in cloud environments such as AWS & Kubernetes

Have experience improving the observability, reliability and performance of complex cloud systems.

Are comfortable with LLM-assisted development and evals.

Are comfortable working with Embedded databases.

Communicate clearly across both technical and non-technical audiences.

Are energized by solving real-world problems for developers

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.

Location independence is the future of work. It serves companies with best talent from around the globe. And benefits employees with a greater work life balance and the ability to work in their pyjamas.- Nithur, founder.

#7308. Vertican: Senior Applied AI Engineer, Document Intelligence

For 40 years Vertican has buildt software that runs law firms. One year ago we created a new modern AI platform for document automation (processing/extraction/redacting) which our customers have been adopting faster than any product we've ever had. Our suite of AI-enabled products save customers thousands of hours each week by processing high volumes of documents with precision and speed.The AI team is growing and we'd like you to join.

We are hiring an engineer to own the document processing pipelines end to end: design, benchmarking, experimenting with new architectures and tools, threshold required for deploying a new pipeline, etc.

We believe it's important to build our own tooling to help us run, improve, and measure our applications. Therefore, the ideal candidate thinks outside the box on how to not just build the product/pipeline, but also the tooling needed around it.

You will be the first dedicated ML hire on the product taking over from the engineering manager who build the current solution and will work directly with the founding engineering team.

This is a senior, hands-on individual-contributor role, but we are small team and you'll be involved in design, planning, roadmapping, and architectural decisions.

Design the pipelines. Own the architecture of the extraction-to-posting pipelines: page classification, field extraction, candidate retrieval, claim matching, confidence gating and the hand-off to human review, etc. Decide where rules end and models begin, and what each stage must guarantee to the next, what the separation of concerns are, etc.

We have different pipelines for different document types and expect that at any given point in time we may have new ones emerge and may consolidate others.

Evaluate new systems before we adopt them. Run structured trials of candidate components and recommend what to keep. Examples include probabilistic record linkage (Splink), Azure AI Content Understanding and Document Intelligence, Azure OpenAI and Foundry-hosted models, and LLM agents with tools. Similarly, test new models with the existing pipeline as they are relased from the frontier labs.

Build the evaluation process. You'll own the process of evaluating false positives, false negatives, errors of ommission, etc, that we ultimately use to determine if we should upgrade the pipeline.

Ship and operate. Take components from trial to production in our python workers on the cloud, instrument them, watch cost and quota, and diagnose the cases reviewers send back.

Set direction. Work with leadereship on the planing for accuracy, cost improvement, and overall product improvements.

Small team, direct access to large datasets, weekly planning, daily standup, everyone remote in US timezones.

No forms. Just smart, optimized applications.

#7307. How Lands’ End’s holiday pop-up feeds an always-on marketing strategy

Sarah Sylvester, who joined the retailer as CMO in March, digs into how a Holiday Tree Farm experience helps meet other imperatives.

Lands’ End is coming home for the holidays this year with an immersive pop-up in Chicago, the city where the Wisconsin-based lifestyle retailer was founded in 1963. The Holiday Tree Farm by Lands’ End is inspired by a line of archival copy that said that the retailer of apparel, outerwear and home furnishings provided “everything but the tree” at Christmastime.

“I just loved what that evoked, as far as being able to sum up how Lands’ End can help you for your holidays, because we literally have something for everybody… I feel like that really embodied what we were trying to accomplish,” CMO Sarah Sylvester said. “Lands’ End has done events in the past for holiday — how can you not? — but we really wanted to make sure that we were challenging everyone to think outside of the box.”

The holiday effort arrives as Lands’ End continues its turnaround efforts. The retailer and brand management firm WHP Global in April completed the creation of a joint venture that is intended to help those efforts. Lands’ End saw net revenue increase 2.7% year over year in Q2 2026 and made moves on marketing spend, customer acquisition and inventory that executives believe will better position it for the back half of the year.

The Holiday Tree Farm, open Nov. 6-8, will feature a variety of seasonally themed experiences, including a tote bag customization station, a vintage photo booth and a cozy lounge. The pop-up’s stations connect with the retailer’s key seasonal product categories — like outerwear and pajamas — so that the experience remains grounded in the brand.

The activation also includes a Holiday Drink Lodge in partnership with coffee chain La Colombe and a Sweater Swap in partnership with resale platform ThredUp that allows guests to exchange a used sweater for a new one.

Lands’ End’s prior collaborations with convenience store chain Wawa and lifestyle brand T&T Twin Talk were cited on a recent earnings call as marketing highlights that helped drive social media traffic and retail sales. But brand collaborations need more than just shared marketer interest, according to Sylvester.

“Even if you have really great brand names, if it doesn't make sense in some way, shape, or form with audience overlap or shared intent, it's hard to make it work,” the executive said. “We want to always ground it back to key product categories, either for us or them… how do you cut through the noise and do something that's fun and disruptive?”

Beyond other brands, Lands’ End is also partnering with Chicago native Lindi Presta and her Running the Runway run club for a “Miles & Monograms” activation and hosting a book reading and signing with local children’s author Lori Orlinsky. The goal of collaborating with such influencers is to help Lands’ End foster stronger local ties and extend the experience beyond Chicago.

“With these types of events, yes, you want to get broad awareness and great social content… even if you can't physically be at the pop-up in Chicago, you can still see and appreciate the content and enjoy it,” Sylvester said. “But specifically with Chicago, I love the localization aspect and how we're really trying to connect with the community.”

Lands’ End’s holiday pop-up arrives at a moment when marketers are reembracing experiential marketing after pandemic disruptions and amid a pendulum swing away from pay-to-play performance marketing toward IRL brand building. The retailer earlier this year teamed with Graduate by Hilton for a “Tote Tailgate Tour” that featured onsite experiences at three college towns.

“I started my career in experiential, so it holds a soft spot in my heart,” Sylvester said. “More than ever, people are craving things in real life and real experiences that they can participate in… There's just something nostalgic and comforting about when you can put down your phone and interact in real life. I think events and experiences are more important than ever.”

For Sylvester, who was appointed in March as Lands’ End’s first chief marketing officer in nearly a decade, experiential is a part of the brand’s always-on marketing — not just an opportunity for a few social posts and possibly PR-driven articles. That approach comes as some marketers are using experiential to help meet other needs around content creation and discoverability in the age of artificial intelligence.

“Experiences cost more than just paying for another paid social ad — how do we make that investment go as far as possible? Yes, it's about, hopefully, the thousands of people that will physically come through and interact with us, but also about creating content for holiday that we can use all season long,” the executive said.

In an increasingly fragmented consumer environment, uniting all aspects of the marketing mix — paid media, paid social, search, influencers, brand collaborations and experiences — around one core brand story is crucial, Sylvester explained.

“I feel like you need these three or four big moments a year of really being clear about who you are and what you stand for,” she said. “In marketing, you’ve got to do a little bit of all of it. It's always an ‘and.’”

#7305. Creators see promise in Instagram’s long-form push, but question whether exposure is enough

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Last week, Instagram said users would be able to post videos up to 90 minutes long, a move aimed at attracting long-form YouTube creators. But, aside from offering a handful of select creators between $50,000 and $500,000 to post long-form content through its new “Features” feature, it has offered no concrete monetization pathway for creators, saying it’s “too early to share a long-form monetization model.”

Early reactions are mixed: Without a clear ad revenue plan, creators may hesitate to make original content for Instagram, while others see promise in the platform’s discovery tools and new TV app.

Digiday spoke with seven creator economy experts about what the change could mean for creators, agents, and marketers.

Instagram is known for reach, but engagement on Reels can vary (marketing software platform Cropink reports Reels get 36% more reach than carousels and 125% more than single-photo posts, but engagement lags). And creators question how that engagement translates into income.

Digital strategist and creator Gabriella Zutrau said that Instagram has great discoverability compared to other platforms, which is helpful for creators looking to build awareness. However, she was “disgusted” that it appears, at least for now, that the platform is “offering discoverability as a form of payment.”

Several of the creator managers and creators Digiday spoke with expressed concerns about the lack of an ad revenue structure for long-form content — especially in comparison to what is widely considered the best platform for earning, YouTube. But it’s increasingly difficult to get monetized on YouTube because of the thresholds required, and it may be harder after its requirements change next year.

“Exposure as a form of payment and with the intention of cutting into YouTube’s market share and cutting into the amount of views people are getting on YouTube — it’s just gross,” Zutrau said. “Instagram has never valued creators.”

Jo Wong, chief revenue officer at agentic AI creator commerce platform POP.STORE, agreed.

“If you talk to most creators, they’ll tell you that that’s really where a lot of the brand deals come through… Instagram in general never really had a super strong monetization program for creators off the get,” said Wong. “Frankly, I think they thought they could get away with [not having a long-form monetization model].”

Samantha Walters, vp of talent experience at Shine Talent Group, said she was “surprised” the platform hadn’t offered a competitive or comparable monetization infrastructure. “That’s why YouTube is so successful, and why creators give so much of their time and effort to it,” she added.

Nick Jacklin, president of Shorthand Studios, was more optimistic, though he emphasized that creators should be paid equitably and transparently, and noted that if Instagram starts running ads on long-form content, it’ll break creator trust.

“I’m all in favor of advocating for a similar model [to YouTube] where Instagram makes $1 [per view], some share of that goes to the creator…but if Instagram can show with the creators that do [Features] work early on that you can drive awareness, I think the monetization will follow,” Jacklin said.

A representative from Instagram told Digiday the platform is “exploring ways for creators to earn from brands, fans and Instagram, including performance-based incentives.”

For Alexandra Mathieu, vp of creative and strategy at Open Influence, the competition Instagram’s long-form push could breed is good for talent. “Instagram putting money behind creator acquisition gives managers more leverage in conversations with YouTube,” she said. “It also opens the door to negotiating beyond fees, including exclusivity, distribution, production support, and creative control.”

With monetization up in the air, are creators and creator agencies planning to do a strategic pivot? It depends on the content genre, it seems.

Mustafa Aijaz, vp of media company SoaR, which works with a lot of gamers and streamers, plans to use it as just another distribution platform for now. “We’ll post our content there, why not? But I’m not making content for Instagram at this moment in time,” he said.

Zack Evans, vp of creative strategy at Shorthand Studios, thinks interview-style shows and scripted comedy could work really well on the platform — especially with the revamped Instagram TV app, which he said promotes scrolling and discoverability more than YouTube does.

“Comedy is inherently surprising, so I think with Reels and Features now, being able to just scroll and see a scripted thing playing out, you don’t yet know what it is, and you’re going to be intrigued,” he said. “It’s much more a pure comedy experience…I think that there will be a certain sector of creators who will actually rise to [this] occasion.”

The ability to pull viewers in with short-form content and funnel them to long-form while remaining on the platform could be a boon for any kind of video creator.

“The top of the funnel is Instagram. [We’re always] trying to get them and trying to use Reels, use Shorts, use any kind of way to grab attention for people, and then try to funnel them over to a longer-form piece of content,” said Evans. “It’s really hard, and these platforms obviously aren’t the best of friends, and so they make it difficult for you to do that.”

The discoverability of short-form and the shareability of content on Instagram could help creators build bigger audiences on the platform, which they can then shop to brands.

Both Wong and Mathieu suggested creators might use Instagram’s Features to recycle older content for reach.

“I’ve encouraged creators to actually use their older content and put it on there,” said Wong. “Most of the views on your YouTube come within the first month, for example, or three weeks, then put your older catalog on Features and test into it. See how your reach is doing. What does it mean for you? Are you able to then get better brand deals, or how does that change?”

She’s advising creators to do that before “catalyzing” original content, whereas Mathieu thinks creators should use Features to use “existing programming as a springboard for new, Instagram-native series such as creator-led shows, movie-length content, longer-running series and video.”

It’s clear that creators are still sussing out Instagram’s latest feature. In a survey sent to 50 of Shine Talent Group’s creators, 93 percent said the platform would have to change its revenue policies to entice them to post long-form content; 100 percent already post on the platform.

Ad tech’s winners and losers emerge as CTV growth masks open-web struggles.

The New York Times, People Inc., News Corp and Reuters report revenue growth despite AI disruption, but face tougher comparisons ahead.

DoorDash’s announced plans to acquire Instacart for $17 billion point to the constant need to gain market share by diversifying services and offerings.
