#7302. Amazon’s shopping agent now takes orders by text, with Sponsored Product ads in the replies

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Amazon’s shopping agent now takes orders by text message and the thread where those orders get placed will carry ads. Sponsored Products are set to appear inside those conversations, free to advertisers for the time being. Purchases the agent completes on its own will stay ad-free.

The texting option went live today for a small, invite-only group of U.S. customers. Amazon plans to open it to millions more over the coming weeks and anyone keen to jump the queue can sign up for the company’s text updates and wait for an invite.

Once in, customers link a phone number, accept the terms and get a new contact called “Alexa Shopping.” From there they message it the way they’d message a friend. Reorder the laundry detergent from last month. Build a grocery shop from a photo of a recipe. Find a birthday present that arrives by Saturday. Alexa for Shopping searches, compares options and texts back with suggestions, then adds the chosen items to the cart and checks out when told to. Customers who’d rather not be asked can switch on auto-buy and let the agent pull the trigger when a price hits their target.

The ad opportunity sits in the discovery stage of that text exchange. When the agent texts back a set of options, a Sponsored Product can be one of them, which puts brands inside one of the most personal channels on a phone with room for a handful of products at most. Amazon has kept ads to that moment, while the customer is still choosing. Slipping one into a purchase the agent makes is a bigger gamble. After all, no one who asks Alexa to reorder dog food when the price drops wants to find out the bag on the doorstep was the one a brand paid for rather than the one they’d have picked.

Amazon isn’t charging for these placements yet, which gives it a cheap way to test how much advertising people will put up with in a text thread — and there’s no shortage of people to test it on. More than 400 million customers have used Alexa for Shopping in the past 12 months, according to Amazon, and use of its agentic features has grown more than eightfold since January.

“Any new ad placement is generally seen as an interesting test and this approach from Amazon is no different,” said independent ad tech consultant Jonathan D’Sousa-Rauto. “Amazon are already well known for putting ad slots on the order confirmation page, which whilst generates ad revenue, could be considered non-incremental for most advertisers. As agentic commerce meets advertising, the definition of an impression is critical as well as the reporting and measurement, to understand if it is truly driving efficiency for brands.”

The feature is designed to work across Amazon’s shopping app, website and Alexa-enabled devices, while those text conversations can carry between them. A request started over text carries into the Amazon Shopping app, the site and Alexa-enabled devices and back again. People can send photos of something spotted while scrolling and ask for lookalikes. They can set price alerts or tell the agent to watch for new launches, with Amazon’s own example being a budget when Nike drops new running shoes. The agent also keeps a running tally of preferences. Mention veganism or marathon training once and it shapes the recommendations that follow. Through Shop Direct, it can also surface and buy products from stores beyond Amazon.

“Customers often jot down what they need the moment they think of it in their notes, in their messages, in their group chats,” said Rajiv Mehta, vp of conversational shopping at Amazon. “Now with Alexa for Shopping, your personal shopping agent is just a text away. Message it what you need and Alexa goes to work on your behalf—searching, comparing, and buying so you can get on with your day.”

This text thread is the latest installment of Amazon’s evolving ad business.

In March, Sponsored Products and Brands prompts arrived, followed by Alexa for Shopping, which replaced Rufus in May and took over the main search bar in the app. This was then followed by Alexa+ Agentic Ads which was announced at Cannes Lions this summer, before the company’s advertiser platform was rebranded as Amazon Ads Agent. During the same month, Amazon blocked Meta’s AI shopping agent Muse, stating that the agent accessed its platform without prior notification, failure to properly identify itself and reportedly captured and stored customers’ details.

A recap of the sessions and insights coming out of Digiday’s Fall Media Buying Summit, which centered on AI’s impact on the media agency world.

In the wake of LiveRamp’s acquisition by Publicis, some marketers are auditioning alternatives to the identity graph company.

Now that Publicis has closed its acquisition of LiveRamp, marketers have a decision to make.

#7294. Horizon3: Staff Technical Support Engineer (Web App)

Horizon3 is a fast-growing, remote cybersecurity company dedicated to the mission of enabling organizations to proactively find and fix and verify exploitable attack vectors before criminals exploit them. Our flagship product, the NodeZeroTM platform, delivers production-safe autonomous pentests and other key assessment operations that scale across the largest internal, external, cloud, and hybrid cloud environments. NodeZero has been adopted by organizations of all sizes, from small educational institutions to government agencies and Global 100 enterprises. It is used by ITOps/SecOps teams, consulting pentesters, and MSSPs and MSPs.

We are a fusion of former U.S. Special Operations cyber operators, startup engineers, and formerly frustrated cybersecurity practitioners. We're committed to helping solve our common security problems: ineffective security tools, false positives resulting in alert fatigue, blind spots, "checkbox” security culture, cybersecurity skills shortage, and the long lead time and expense of hiring outside consultants. Collectively, we are a team of learn it alls, committed to a culture of respect, collaboration, ownership, and results.

Investigate complex failures across the entire stack. A single case might take you from Kubernetes pod scheduling, through container networking, into cloud IAM permissions, and into our application logs in Datadog. You own the investigation end to end.

Be the technical bridge between customers and engineering. You'll translate customer-reported symptoms into actionable bug reports with reproduction steps, logs, and root cause hypotheses. When engineering ships a fix, you verify it actually solves the problem in the customer's environment.

Build tooling and automation. If you're doing something manually more than twice, you build a tool. We need someone who writes Python scripts, builds internal dashboards, automates data collection from Zendesk and Jira, and creates runbooks that make the whole team more effective.

Drive systemic improvements. You'll spot patterns across tickets that point to product gaps, documentation holes, or infrastructure reliability issues. You bring the data, make the case, and work with engineering and product to fix the root cause, not just the symptom.

Go deep on web application security. Our platform now does web app pentesting. You need to understand common vulnerability classes (OWASP top 10), how web scanners work, and how to explain findings and their implications to customers who range from security experts to IT generalists.

Mentor the team and raise the technical bar. You'll help junior and senior engineers develop their troubleshooting skills, create training content, and establish patterns for how the team approaches complex investigations.

8+ years of hands-on technical experience. The title on your resume matters less than what you've actually done. We'll take an SRE who built incident response tooling, a backend developer who ran production infrastructure, or a support engineer who can write real code and read Terraform.

Strong Linux fundamentals. You're comfortable on a command line, can troubleshoot a hung process on RHEL, understand SELinux, and don't need a GUI to be productive.

Container and Kubernetes experience. You've deployed and troubleshot containerized workloads. You know the difference between a CrashLoopBackOff and an ImagePullBackOff and what to do about each.

Cloud platform experience (AWS, Azure, or GCP). You understand IAM, networking (VPCs, security groups, firewalls), and have worked with Infrastructure as Code tools (Terraform, Ansible, or CloudFormation) -- not just reading plans, but writing and debugging them when provisioning fails.

GraphQL and web application fluency. Our API is GraphQL. You can trace a request from a React frontend through the GraphQL layer to the backend, interpret query/mutation responses (including partial errors in a 200), and debug auth flows, CORS issues, and network failures using browser dev tools.

Database fundamentals. You can write SQL to investigate data issues, understand relational and NoSQL concepts, and ideally have exposure to graph databases -- our product maps attack paths and relationships that are naturally graph-shaped.

Scripting ability in Python and Bash. You've built automation that other people actually use, not just one-off scripts for yourself.

Observability tool experience (Datadog preferred). You can search logs, build queries, read traces, and correlate signals across services to find where something broke.

Strong communication skills. You'll write to customers, file bugs for engineering, and present findings to product. Each audience needs a different approach and you adjust naturally.

Background in cybersecurity, pentesting, or AppSec. Understanding of common exploit classes, vulnerability assessment, and security tooling.

Experience with web application security. Familiar with XSS, SQLi, SSRF, auth bypass, IDOR, and how WAFs and CDNs affect security testing.

Active Directory experience. Many of our customers' environments are AD-heavy, and NodeZero tests AD attack paths (password audits, credential attacks, privilege escalation). Understanding AD architecture, GPOs, trusts, and common misconfigurations is a real advantage.

Full-stack development experience. You've shipped production code in Python, JavaScript/TypeScript, or Go.

Deep graph database experience (Neo4j or similar). Our attack path data is graph-native -- if you can write Cypher queries and understand graph traversal, you'll hit the ground running.

Certifications (AWS SA Pro, CKA, RHCE, OSCP, or similar). Not required, but signals self-direction and depth.

Experience in regulated environments (SOC 2, FedRAMP, GDPR). Useful given our customer base.

We are a fully remote company, and this job may require up to 5% of travel to be successful.

Inclusive Team: We value diversity and promote an inclusive culture where everyone can thrive.

Growth Opportunities: Be part of a dynamic and growing team with numerous career development opportunities.

Innovative Culture: Work in a collaborative environment that encourages creativity and out-of-the-box thinking.

Hybrid & Remote Work: We embrace a mix of remote and hybrid work models depending on role and location, including our Chicago office, where some roles require regular in-office presence.

#7291. Political Campaigns Spent a Near-Record $331 Per Voter, But Waste Forces Rethink

Where are the most expensive media markets in the U.S.? At least for political campaigns in 2026, they were Maine, Alaska, and Iowa.

These thinly populated battleground states saw spending of up to $331 per registered voter for the 2026 election cycle, according to data from AdImpact and state voter rolls.

That came as total political spending hit a record $11.9 billion across TV, digital, and audio, exceeding the 2024 presidential year’s $11.2 billion. None of that includes substantial direct mail or outdoor campaigns.

Maine saw the highest spending per voter at $331, followed by Alaska at $313 and Iowa at $252. They fell just shy of a record set by Montana in 2024, which got $335 per voter thanks to a competitive Senate seat. Competitive Senate and gubernatorial races fueled a record number of three-digit outlays per voter in 2026, including Ohio ($112) and Kansas ($102).

Spending per voter doubled and even tripled from two years ago in all five triple-digit-spending states. Republicans outspent Democrats in four of those five but lost key races in all of them, per AdImpact, with the Alaska Senate race still being counted.

That so much money failed to change outcomes could force a major rethink of 2028 strategy.

Heavy spending meant local ads spilling into national buys. By October, Senate campaign ads from Iowa, Texas, Florida, Maine, and Kansas ran on some national broadcasts of NCAA and NFL football games.

A shift to digital and connected TV spending this year made targeting theoretically easier, but didn’t guarantee reaching the right people.

Sal Tuzzeo, a Fabric Media executive based near Cleveland, said his 8-year-old son was bombarded by political ads, even though he was watching age-appropriate content on the big screen from YouTube creators TerraGreen, The Fish Guys, and Unspeakable.

“The amount of political money flowing into streaming and CTV seemed absurd,” Tuzzeo said. “The mistargeting was egregious.” He attributed it to “a broad reach land grab coupled with overt aversion to frequency capping.”

Over the past few months, nearly every ad in every pod his son watched was political, Tuzzeo said. His child could recite verbatim ads dealing with abortion, sex changes, crack cocaine, and domestic violence.

Republicans outspent Democrats by a $5.5 billion to $5 billion margin nationwide, per AdImpact. But that advantage made little difference. For example, of 87 candidates backed by Elon Musk’s America PAC, 80 lost, according to Travis Trawick, CEO of nonpartisan election tech firm FullPAC.

Too much money went into broadcast over the final five weeks, when time is most expensive and when “at least in this election, most people’s minds were already made up,” Trawick said. “Running the same ad in front of people 60 times just has diminishing returns.”

For 2028, he believes campaigns will shift spending earlier in the cycle and toward AI-driven digital and social strategies, using AI not just to optimize media but also to collect return path signals and shape creative that spawns voter engagement.

“Being able to create a conversation between a voter and a candidate or an issue, not just talking at them, and doing that at scale, is something that’s never really been possible before,” Trawick said. “Now it is.”

#7288. Senior ML Engineer - Malware Detection (remote work) at CloudLinux

CloudLinux is a global remote-first company. We are driven by our principles: do the right thing, employees first, we are remote first, and we deliver high-volume, low-cost Linux infrastructure and security products that help companies to increase the efficiency of their operations. Every person on our team supports each other and does what we can to ensure we all are successful.

Check out our website for more information: https://cloudlinux.com/

Imunify360 is CloudLinux's security suite for Linux web servers: real-time and background malware scanning, automated cleanup, a WAF, runtime PHP protection (Proactive Defense), a database malware scanner, and IP reputation. It runs on hosting providers' shared, VPS, and dedicated servers, so new web-malware campaigns land on our fleet early.

We sit on one of the largest labeled corpora of live web-server malware in the industry: files, verdicts, cleanup outcomes, and analyst decisions collected from production hosting servers every day. It already drives our signatures, heuristics, and ML models, and there is far more to get out of it. We are looking for a Senior ML Engineer who will turn that data into detection that ships — classifiers, LLM-assisted triage and generated signatures — and keep exploring new ML/LLM methods against it, measured by the product's false-negative and false-positive rates, not by notebook accuracy.

Our dataset focuses on web malware — PHP, JavaScript, HTML phishing kits, infected WordPress and Joomla plugins and themes, database injections — with the context around each file.

The goal is 10x more malware detected at the same or lower false-positive rate. Hand-written rules and faster pipelines will not get there; models trained on our data might.

You get an experimentation track, GPUs, and a direct line to leadership; in return, you bring numbers, not opinions.

You own the path from raw samples to a model in production, and the numbers that prove it helped.

#7283. Senior ML Engineer - Email Security (remote work) at CloudLinux

CloudLinux is a global remote-first company. We are driven by our principles: do the right thing, employees first, we are remote first, and we deliver high-volume, low-cost Linux infrastructure and security products that help companies to increase the efficiency of their operations. Check out our website for more information: https://cloudlinux.com/

CloudLinux Inc. develops the Imunify family of security products, designed to protect hosting environments from a wide range of cyber threats. One of these solutions is Imunify Email, an email security product that protects hosting servers against spam, phishing, and other email-based threats.

Imunify Email filters mail on about 5,000 hosting servers. Today the filter is rspamd with rules and a scoring model we train in-house. We are replacing it with a transformer we trained ourselves that runs on the customer's own CPU in under 100 MB and already beats rspamd at every operating point we measured. We are looking for a Senior Machine Learning Engineer to join our team and work alongside the engineer currently responsible for both the existing spam-filtering pipeline and the new ML model. Together, you will drive the development, improvement, and production rollout of our email security technology.

#7263. Brandweek 2026: The ‘Why’ Test Behind Mars’ Boldest Ideas ft. Rankin Carroll

The best creative risks should make you a little nervous. But they should never make you ask why your brand is taking them.

Live from the Marketing Vanguard stage at Brandweek 2026, Jenny Rooney sits down with Rankin Carroll, chief brand officer of Mars Snacking, to unpack how brands like M&M’s, Snickers and Skittles stay culturally relevant without treating boldness as an excuse for chaos.

Rankin takes us behind the scenes of the 2023 Snickers AI experiment that felt “potentially crazy” at the time.

He also gets candid about Mars’ creator learning curve and the responsibility marketers have to the next generation.

This episode is part of a special vidcast series that was recorded live during Brandweek 2026 and presented in partnership with Tatari.

Rankin Carroll is Chief Brand Officer of Mars Snacking, where he works across some of the company’s most recognizable brands, including M&M’s, Snickers and Skittles. He has spent more than 20 years at Mars across marketing, advertising, general management and global and regional leadership roles. His career has taken him from Toronto to the UK and at one point, included running an ice cream business along the way.

Today, Rankin focuses on what he describes as the stewardship of distinctive, iconic brands: protect what makes them valuable, make them stronger than when you received them, and still create enough tension to keep them culturally relevant.

[04:33] The Snickers AI Bet That Looked “Potentially Crazy” — In 2023, Mars put Snickers into an agentic creative experience at a point when the safety and reputational risks looked far less understood than they do today. The project involved roughly ten tech partners, legal and security leadership, and a list of 8,000 blocked words. The takeaway is much bigger than “take more risk.” It’s that ambitious experimentation becomes possible when the organization commits to solving the risk together.

[07:56] Earn the Right to Take Risks — Rankin draws a hard line between calculated cultural risk and performative brand behavior. Skittles could maintain its relationship with the LGBTQ+ community during a more volatile period because the brand had spent years building credibility there. His filter for every big swing is simple: Why us? Why this brand? If there is no strategically convincing answer, the idea does not deserve the risk.

[13:42] Are We Preparing the Next Generation for What Marketing Becomes Next? — Rankin raises one of the more uncomfortable questions from Brandweek 2026: what happens to junior marketers when the profession changes around AI? He expects the practice to look completely different within three years and openly says he does not yet have a satisfying answer for how marketers entering the industry should be trained. For him, that makes talent development a leadership responsibility, not an HR afterthought.

[15:03] “A Thousand Pilots and No Progress” — Rankin believes leaving AI adoption to disconnected enthusiasts across an organization is a recipe for chaos. CMOs need to own the agenda alongside technology leadership, bringing top-down clarity to investment, priorities and what the company should build, buy, rent or borrow. His warning is simple: just because you can use AI somewhere does not mean you should.

Jenny Rooney is Chief Brand and Community Officer, leading strategy for the overall ADWEEK brand as well as the ways in which we serve and support our audiences with high-value content, products, partnerships and experiences, notably through our community programs such as Marketing Vanguard.

#7257. Meerkat’s Dustin Black Makes an Art of Staying Curious

Creative energy doesn’t ebb when the world stops. So when the pandemic stalled agency work in 2020, Dustin Black turned his lifelong pursuit of creativity on his home.

The career art director—recently named chief creative officer and partner at full-service Minneapolis creative agency Meerkat (formerly Preston Spire)—has twin daughters, who were 6 years old at the time. He’d been planning to turn their family mantra—“Be brave, be curious, be kind”—into a giant banner for their home, and suddenly had the time to make it.

Using felt sourced from New York-based manufacturer Oxford Pennant, Black’s banner infused some much-needed cheer into their basement. He also shared the finished product with the company.

“Oxford Pennant just absolutely loved it,” he said, so much so that they began producing a pennant using Black’s design—and offered him a supply of felt for future projects.

In fact, Black did have another idea in the works: He’d been sculpting a 5-foot-long fish out of foam that was in need of scales. As it happened, at-home cutting machine maker Cricut had also taken an interest in Black’s banner and offered to sponsor his work.

All that was left was for Black to take every hands-on art lesson, from fingerpainting as a toddler to his double major in art at the University of Nebraska-Lincoln, and apply it to a new kind of blank slate. “I had to figure out, what would a felt fish look like? I started playing, and I suddenly had my first fish covered in felt scales,” he said.

Black has been chasing new creative highs ever since, from making ever-more complex scales to cladding his fish in felt flowers instead, then turning to sculpting birds. Each sculpture begins with elaborate sketches, then Black figures out the size and frames it out in metal, wood, and foam.

The Cricut does the tedious work of cutting out the felt, then Black glues on each piece and further decorates it with paint and other materials. Everything is done inside his 25-square-foot home office on nights and weekends.

Since the beginning, he’s involved his girls to “show them it’s OK to try something new and fail” (they now have their own art projects as well). Black also documents the creative process of each piece on Instagram, including his mistakes and how he moved on from them.

“I’m trying to put out there the notion of we’re all beginners, and we’re all figuring it out, and it might look good in the end but it took a lot to get there,” he explained.

His sculptures have been exhibited in galleries, at events and, most recently, for a year at Minneapolis-Saint Paul International Airport. Having them in a public space “has been a lot of fun because I get random texts from people: ‘Look at what my family group chat is talking about!’” he said.

The pursuit of new shapes, styles, and designs is what drives Black, which is why he’s resisted taking commissions that would dictate what he makes—or worse, copy his previous work.

His latest project, an eagle with feathers made of polymer clay that Black hand-painted, has pride of place inside the Meerkat offices. With the widespread suspicion of AI in creative work, Black knows the value in clients being able to see and touch a piece of art made in-house.

He also hopes it inspires the kind of creativity that drives good work: “We need to be doing new things. We need to be solving complex problems. And we need to use curiosity to approach it.”

Off the Clock chronicles the hobbies, sports, and side hustles of people in the advertising and marketing world. We always welcome your nominations at eva.kis@adweek.com.

Eva Kis is the production editor at Adweek.

#7250. Fox Corp. Completes $22 Billion Deal for Roku

There is a new sheriff in Roku City: Fox has completed its purchase of the streaming and tech company.

The $22 billion deal closed today, combining Fox Corp.’s broadcast, news, sports, streaming, and local news portfolio with Roku’s open connected TV platform and first-party data from more than 100 million global streaming households.

The merged company becomes the third-largest entity in U.S. television by share of viewing. Fox says the addition of Roku will create new opportunities to serve its viewers, advertisers, and partners.

Executive chair and CEO of Fox Corp. Lachlan Murdoch said acquiring Roku advances the media company’s long-term strategy and will continue to “relentlessly serve audiences and partners, while reinforcing the openness and choice that is the essence of the Roku platform.”

Roku will be folded into Fox Digital Platforms, which also houses fellow FAST service Tubi, Fox One, and Red Seat Ventures. The streaming platform’s founder, Anthony Wood, will join Fox’s board of directors and serve as co-chair of Fox Digital Platforms alongside Paul Cheesbrough, the unit’s CEO.

When the merger was announced in June, the companies said they were looking to create a “scaled next-generation media and technology company” to prosper in today’s environment of increasing consumption of live sports, news, and streaming.

Roku stockholders will receive $95.30 in cash and 0.9799 shares of Fox Class A common stock for each share of Roku Class A and Roku Class B common stock they owned before the deal closed, under the merger agreement.

Separately, Fox shareholders will now own approximately 74% of the outstanding shares of Fox common stock, and the remaining approximately 26% will go to former Roku shareholders.

Reflecting on Roku’s acquisition, Woods said in a statement that he was proud of what his team has built and that combining with Fox provides an opportunity to accelerate its vision, scale faster, and be more aggressive in innovation for its respective audiences.

Woods added, “I couldn’t be more excited about what we’ll accomplish together.”

#7248. Container Engineer at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth. More than 9 million developers and 250,000+ customers now build on Supabase, and the majority of new databases on the platform are created by AI app builders — so the systems underneath have to stay reliable while they grow fast.

Container Engineering (ConEng) owns the internal container platform that runs Supabase's multi-tenant, multi-region services — the managed Kubernetes platform that other engineering teams build and deploy on top of. We own cluster lifecycle and cluster classes, multi-cluster and multi-region topology, capacity, scheduling, isolation and runtime safety, the higher-level platform APIs, the Kubernetes deployment contract and Argo semantics, rollout primitives, and runtime configuration and secret injection.

Your job is to make the platform itself trustworthy, observable, and cheap to operate at scale — and to raise the reliability bar for everyone who depends on it. This is not a gatekeeper role. You'll make the reliable path the easy path: building the automation and platform APIs that let product teams run safely by default, instrumenting what we operate, and making sure that when something breaks we detect it and recover fast.

Tooling & ecosystem — deep knowledge of the container and orchestration landscape (Kubernetes, containerd/OCI, the CNCF ecosystem) and a clear point of view on how to compose it.

Software engineering — designing and building the platform's automation, controllers, and internal tooling as production code, including solutions that don't come pre-shaped by an existing tool.

Operational / SRE practice — the discipline (SLOs, error budgets, incident response, ORRs) that runs the platform at scale as a predictable one.

We expect you to be genuinely deep in at least one of these, and many of our strongest candidates are deep in two (usually leaning toward one). Bringing real strength across all three is rare and exceptional — and even then, no one is equally strong in all three at once, so you'd focus where you're most needed at any given time. Different profiles are welcome here; tell us which of these is your center of gravity.

Regardless of the skill set, all team members carry a share of the team's on-call rota, responding to platform incidents and driving them to resolution and follow-up.

Define SLIs/SLOs and error budgets for the container platform itself, and build error-budget policies that actually gate platform changes.

Own the Operational Readiness Review (ORR) process for new platform capabilities and major changes.

Design and track metrics for platform uptime, and increase observability into the platform by capturing the right metrics, logs, and traces across the platform layers.

Drive the incident-to-improvement pipeline: facilitate blameless postmortems, connect findings to readiness gaps, spot repeat failure patterns, and push through systemic fixes.

Build and maintain the platform's automation, controllers, operators, and internal tooling — writing production code and shipping it, not just wiring together existing tools.

Design and implement the platform-side CI/CD and rollout primitives that speed up platform deployments while working within proper change and release management.

Implement and maintain security automation for the platform: intrusion detection, automated remediation, and patch management.

Identify and quantify operational toil, and build the automation that removes it rather than just documenting it.

Contribute to the platform's continual testing automation — the test suites and pipelines that validate platform releases are fit for purpose before they reach tenants.

Act as a platform reliability and architecture expert — contribute to failure-mode analysis, dependency mapping, and resilience design for multi-tenant container workloads.

Evaluate and evolve the platform's tooling ecosystem (orchestration, runtime, networking, isolation, and observability stack) with a clear, opinionated view on trade-offs.

Improve system architecture to reduce infrastructure cost while balancing security, reliability, and performance across tenants.

Guide teams building on the platform to design sustainable on-call practices for their own services: alert quality, escalation paths, runbook coverage, and noise reduction.

Have 5+ years in platform engineering, container/Kubernetes platform operations, SRE, or production infrastructure, and have operated production systems at scale and carried on-call for them.

Are genuinely deep in at least one — ideally two — of the three strength areas below (and tell us which is your center of gravity):

Tooling & ecosystem — know the container/orchestration landscape well: Kubernetes, containerd and the OCI/CRI standards, and the surrounding CNCF ecosystem (e.g. Argo CD/Argo Rollouts for GitOps and progressive delivery; Cilium/eBPF networking and the Gateway API; Karpenter/KEDA-style autoscaling; and workload-isolation runtimes such as gVisor or Kata Containers). You reason about trade-offs rather than reaching for defaults.

Software engineering — write real production code (Go and/or a comparable systems language) and can build controllers, operators, and platform APIs — stepping back to the underlying problem and building the right solution when no off-the-shelf tool fits.

Operational / SRE practice — are fluent in SLOs, error budgets, and operational KPIs; have led incident response and blameless postmortems; and have driven down MTTD/MTTR for systems you operated.

Have worked with large-scale multi-tenant systems and understand isolation as a spectrum — from namespaces, RBAC, network policies, and resource quotas through to stronger primitives such as virtual clusters (e.g. vCluster) or VM-class runtimes.

Operate confidently on AWS (multiple accounts, IAM, VPC) and are comfortable with infrastructure-as-code (Pulumi preferred; Terraform/CDK also fine) and Kubernetes.

Script and automate to eliminate toil rather than absorb it.

Communicate clearly with both infrastructure specialists and product engineers, and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and iterating toward better systems over time.

Experience building and operating a multi-tenant Kubernetes platform that internal teams deploy onto.

Familiarity with policy engines (Kyverno, or OPA/Gatekeeper) and cost-attribution tooling (OpenCost/Kubecost) for per-tenant chargeback/showback.

Experience with managed database platforms or Postgres.

Familiarity with OpenTelemetry, Prometheus/VictoriaMetrics, Grafana, or similar observability tooling.

Xapo is a bank, and a bank earns on the money its members choose to hold with it. Each of those products shipped well. What hasn't yet existed is a single owner of the economics of the customer relationship: one person accountable for winning the right members, growing what they hold with us, and keeping them, and for the balance-sheet products that make that possible.

The Chief Customer Officer is a new role at the top of the house, reporting to the CEO. You will run the stream that grows what members hold with Xapo, and you will own the Customer P&L, retail and institutional. It brings growth, client coverage, product management, and product design under one leader who is judged on customer contribution, the assets attracted and kept, and the members who stay. This is a customer-focused role in the most commercial sense: what a member earns from us, not the feature we shipped, is the centre of every decision.

The measure of success is a Customer P&L that grows on what members hold and keep: LTV:CAC improving, approved-to-paid rising, churn falling, and customer contribution the business can plan against.

This is a banking role, not a requirements-gathering role, a marketing role, or a role you can run from a dashboard. Products are priced on the balance sheet and judged on the money they attract and keep.

This part matters as much as the mandate. The known failure mode for this role, in any company, is a CCO with a polished customer-insight deck and no ownership of the number. That is disqualifying here.

Day 30: The Customer P&L baseline is documented across retail and institutional: customer contribution, LTV:CAC, approved-to-paid, churn, net paid-member growth, and assets attracted and kept. You have formed your own independent view of where members are won, lost, and under-served, and shared it with the CEO and Board.Day 60: The unified Customer operating model is live: one set of key results across Growth, Client Coverage, Mission PMs, and Product Design, read weekly from one source. Every mission PM owns a product's contribution and its price, and the roadmap is ranked on what it earns, built with Finance and the line leads.Day 90: At least one measurable improvement in LTV:CAC, approved-to-paid, churn, or net new AUM flow is live and attributable to a change you made, not a feature ship, an outcome moved. Every cell in your line has defined key results and an owner who explains the number every week.

#7232. #AskNewser: CNN, NBC News and NewsNation Executives Reflect on Their Election Night Coverage

As the dust settles from a historic midterm election that saw the Democratic Party surf a blue wave to capture both the Senate and the House, news outlets are downshifting from special coverage and settling back into regular programming.

With 31 million viewers tuning in Tuesday night across 13 networks, TVNewser recently spoke to NBC News’ evp, creative, innovation & field production Marc Greenstein, whose network was the No.1 network during primetime in total viewers and the Adults 25-54 demo, as well as CNN’s svp, Washington & special events programming Abigail Crutchfield, whose network was the most-watched cable news network in the prized demo, and NewsNation’s political director Chris Stirewalt, whose network in coordination with Decision Desk HQ was the first one to call both the House and the Senate for the Democrats, for a post-election AskNewser on what worked well during their live election coverage.

TVNewser: What worked well from a coverage and technology standpoint on election night?

Greenstein: Steve Kornacki continues to be the best at breaking down the vote, providing context and helping viewers understand not just what is happening, but where the night is headed.

We combined editorial and technology in a new way, allowing Steve to contribute to the linear broadcast while simultaneously hosting his own dedicated streaming feed from the same studio as Savannah Guthrie and Tom Llamas. This allowed us to produce two distinct broadcasts just feet apart, each tailored to its audience, without losing the editorial connection between our talent.

Extensive planning for multiple election scenarios allowed us to react quickly as race calls came in. We deployed multiple correspondents across nearly every battleground state, covering both voting developments and candidate headquarters. This gave us a strong presence in the places driving the story and allowed us to bring firsthand reporting and reaction into the broadcast throughout the night.

We played more than 35,000 graphics across our zones and platforms, delivering results and analysis with speed and clarity. We brought together broadcast, streaming and digital coverage under a consistent editorial and visual approach, while tailoring the experience to each platform. The launch of our dedicated vertical video feed was another important step in reaching audiences in new ways without compromising the depth or quality of our journalism.

Cruthchfield: It was having all of our experts and talent together in one place. It gave the DC bureau great energy, and it was fun to watch the reporters and anchors work together to unpack the results.

Our director, Reza Baktar, designed a phenomenal set that gave us more mobility than ever and made it easy to get to any data set we needed at any moment. I also loved giving John King the ability to analyze the data at the magic wall or to bring his mini magic wall over to our analyst tables to be a part of the conversation. And I loved King’s new vertical magic wall, which opened up new storytelling options.

Stirewalt: You can’t beat the cards. We had such an advantage by having Decision Desk HQ (DDHQ) on our side. I think we did a good job of bringing viewers into the process and explaining what we were doing, but when you get down to it, the advantage of being so far ahead of the competition was just amazing.

TN: What was the biggest surprise of the night?

Greenstein: The biggest surprise was how quickly we were able to call control of the Senate. While there was a general sense that it could be a good night for Democrats, I think the early call caught many of us a little off guard. That said, we had planned for a wide range of scenarios and were prepared to stay on air throughout the night across the broadcast network, NBC News Now and Kornacki Cam.

Another pleasant surprise was how well the AI audio technology worked, allowing us to produce the linear and streaming broadcasts simultaneously from the same studio. We had limited time to rehearse with the technology, so there were certainly some nerves heading into the night. But it performed remarkably well and allowed us to pull off something we had never done before.

Cruthchfield: I think perhaps how quickly we were able to project the major results of the night. We always plan for every scenario, but this Tuesday we got answers from the voters quickly.

Stirewalt: How much work I wasted preparing for close elections in so many places. I was buckled in for tight contests in battleground states. We found out by 8:30 p.m. ET or so that what we really needed to get good at was going deep on places like Mississippi.

TN: Share the tricks you used to keep yourself awake and alert on Tuesday night.

Greenstein: Coffee, Diet Coke, and the adrenaline of being in the director’s chair! In my role, I rarely get the opportunity to direct. The last time was Election Night 2024. So getting back in the chair and working alongside the most talented people in the business, both on air and behind the scenes, pushes me to be at the top of my game. And I love every minute of it.

Cruthchfield: A lot of hot tea, a phenomenal team, and a big bag of gummy bears.

Stirewalt: I don’t need any outside stimulants. When the results started pouring in, I was so thrilled at how DDHQ and our team were doing that I was just high on life.

#7225. Ticker: Carson Daly Named Saturday Today Co-Anchor

Top of the Ticker: NBC News has named Carson Daly the co-anchor of Saturday Today, beginning Saturday, November 7. Daly will continue his weekday role, expanding his duties at NBC News to six days a week.

“I’m thrilled to add Saturday Today to my weekday morning schedule,” said Daly, who will join co-anchor Laura Jarrett, feature anchor Joe Fryer, and meteorologist Angie Lassman.

Daly, who joined Today as host of the Orange Room in 2013, fills the void left by Peter Alexander, who left the program in March and later left NBC News after more than 20 years to join MS NOW.

Revolving Door: CNBC announced it has signed a new long-term agreement with Jim Cramer, which includes launching a premium subscription tier of his CNBC Investing Club in 2027.

The business network also recently announced some executive moves affecting its dayside programming and editorial operations.

Vice president of programming and senior executive producer Todd Bonin will now lead Business Day from 5 a.m. to noon ET. Sandy Cannold, vp of programming and sep will now oversee the network’s programming from noon to 6 p.m. ET.

Meanwhile, Jason Gewirtz moves to the Digital Control Room, while Anne Tironi will continue to oversee Squawk Box and Lisa Villalobos will manage all three hours of Squawk on the Street. Both will report to Bonin. In addition to leading Closing Bell: Overtime,  Maria Boden will take on Fast Money. Matt Quayle will move from sep of The Exchange and Power Lunch to a new role focused on content development across platforms.

On the newsgathering and reporting front, Lacy O’Toole, vp and managing editor, will also add the network’s Specials unit to her docket. All correspondents will now report directly to O’Toole, except those aligned with the strategic verticals led by Max Meyers. In addition, correspondents based in Washington, D.C. and San Francisco will report to MC Wellons and Christina Passariello, respectively. Finally, CNBC’s Investigations unit will fall under the purview of Ken Brown, with Scott Zamost and Paige Tortorelli reporting to him.

Brand refresh: MS NOW has introduced a new brand campaign titled “Now There’s More That Unites US.” The network says this campaign was inspired by “the groundswell of participation, with millions of individuals making their voices heard, standing up for what they believe in.”
